] were carried
out using the MD simulation scheme described in
Section III.2
,
for parameters
Eq. (212)
, in a cluster of linear
dimension
L=96
(
N=9216
spins).
Both simulation results and analytic prediction have been convoluted
with a Gaussian of
\text{FWHM}=0.02\ J
.

VII
Quantum–Classical correspondence

The reason why “raw” results of molecular dynamics simulations, presented in
Section VI.4
,
capture the dispersion of quantum excitations,
while failing to describe their spectral weight, is rooted in the
classical statistics of the underlying classical Monte Carlo simulations.

The equation of motions (EoM) on which the u3MD is based,
Eq. (73)
,
correctly describe the dynamics of a
spin–1
moment, at a semi–classical level.
And, solved analytically, with appropriate quantization, these EoM yield identical results
to the linear multiple–Boson expansion developed in

Section V

Remund
.
However, the spectral weight found in u3MD simulation is not subject
to any quantization condition.
Instead this is determined by thermal fluctuations, subject to the
classical statistics of Monte Carlo simulation.
And for this reason, all spectral weight vanishes for
T\to 0
, as thermal
fluctuations are eliminated, cf.
Fig. 17
.

None the less, the fact that spectral weight is concentrated in a single peak,
with Lorentzian lineshape, that becomes arbitrarily sharp
for
T\to 0
, suggests that low–temperature simulation results
can be understood within a single–mode approximation.
And this encourages us to believe that it may be possible to “undo”
the effect of classical statistics, in limit
T\to 0
.
This line of reasoning, developed below, leads to a simple
prescription for correcting MD simulation results

\displaystyle S^{\sf QM}({\bf q},\omega,T=0)=\lim_{T\to 0}\frac{\hbar\omega}{k_{B}T}S^{\sf MD}({\bf q},\omega,T)\;,

(225)

previously introduced in
Eq. (12)
.
This prescription is shown to restore to perfect agreement with
zero–temperature quantum results, at a semi–classical level.

In what follows, we set out this analysis in more detail.
In
Section VII.1
, we
“deconstruct” the dynamical structure factors
found in u3MD simulations, analysing their intensities in terms of
excitations with classical statistics, while retaining the quantum
(more precisely, semi–classical) nature of their dynamics.
Using what we have learned, in
Section VII.2
,
we show explicitly that u3MD simulation results can be corrected
to yield dynamical structure factors in agreement with the predictions
of
Section V
.
We conclude, in
Section VII.3
, with a comparison
of u3MC and u3MD simulations with published results from
Quantum Monte Carlo (QMC).

VII.1
Molecular dynamics, deconstructed

We start by exploring
the relationship between classical
and quantum theories for fluctuations about FQ order, and their
implication for the understanding of simulation.
We concentrate on the experimentally–relevant structure factor
for dipole moments
S_{\sf S}(\textbf{q})
, re–deriving the classical
result quoted in
Section IV

in a framework which permits direct comparison with the quantum
result given in
Section V
.

We take as starting point the quantum theory of excitations about the FQ state,

Eq. (184)
, and include a term
\Delta{\cal H}[{\bf h}]

describing coupling of dipole moments to a transverse field

\displaystyle{\cal H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\cal H}^{\prime}_{\sf BBQ}+\Delta{\cal H}[{\bf h}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{0}+\Delta E_{0}+\sum_{\textbf{k}}\hbar\epsilon(\textbf{k})\left[{\hat{\alpha}_{\textbf{k}}^{\dagger}}{\hat{\alpha}_{\textbf{k}}}+{\hat{\beta}_{\textbf{k}}^{\dagger}}{\hat{\beta}_{\textbf{k}}}\right]

\displaystyle-\sum_{\textbf{k}}\xi_{\sf S}(\textbf{k})\left[ih^{x}_{\textbf{k}}({\hat{\beta}_{\textbf{k}}}-{\hat{\beta}_{-\textbf{k}}^{\dagger}})+ih^{z}_{\textbf{k}}({\hat{\alpha}_{-\textbf{k}}^{\dagger}}-{\hat{\alpha}_{\textbf{k}}})\right]\;,

where the excitation energy
\epsilon(\textbf{k})
is defined through

Eq. (185)
,
\xi_{\sf S}(\textbf{k})
is the coherence factor
defined in
Eq. (192)
, and all terms at
cubic and higher order in Bosons have been neglected.
Here and in what follows we restore dimensional constants
\hbar
and

k_{B}
, which have been set to unity elsewhere.

Recognising
as the Hamiltonian for a set of independent
simple harmonic oscillators (SHO), we introduce a new set of coordinates

\displaystyle{\hat{\alpha}_{\textbf{k}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{m\epsilon(\textbf{k})}{2\hbar}}\hat{x}_{1,\textbf{k}}+\frac{i}{\sqrt{2\hbar m\epsilon(\textbf{k})}}\hat{p}_{1,\textbf{k}}\;,

(227a)

\displaystyle{\hat{\beta}_{\textbf{k}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{m\epsilon(\textbf{k})}{2\hbar}}\hat{x}_{2,\textbf{k}}+\frac{i}{\sqrt{2\hbar m\epsilon(\textbf{k})}}\hat{p}_{2,\textbf{k}}\;,

(227b)

satisfying the canonical commutation relation

\displaystyle[\hat{x}_{\nu,\textbf{k}},\hat{p}_{\nu^{\prime},\textbf{k}^{\prime}}]=i\hbar\delta_{\textbf{k}\textbf{k}^{\prime}}\delta_{\nu\nu^{\prime}}\;.

(228)

with
\nu=1,2
.
Written in terms of these coordinates, the Hamiltonian
[
] becomes

\displaystyle{\cal H}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{0}+\Delta E_{0}

(229)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\nu,\textbf{k}}\left[\frac{m\epsilon(\textbf{k})^{2}}{2}\hat{x}_{\nu,\textbf{k}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2m}\hat{p}_{\nu,\textbf{k}}^{2}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad-\left.\sqrt{\frac{2\xi_{\sf S}^{2}(\textbf{k})}{m\hbar\epsilon(\textbf{k})}}\left(h^{z}_{\textbf{k}}\delta_{1,\nu}+h^{x}_{\textbf{k}}\delta_{2,\nu}\right)\hat{p}_{\nu,\textbf{k}}\right]\;,

As long as the commutation relation,
Eq. (228)
, is respected,
the excitations of
Eq. (229)
continue to have well–defined, Bosons statistics.
Meanwhile, the neglect of higher–order terms means that the dynamics of
these excitations are treated at the level of a semi–classical approximation.
MD simulation, on the other hand, imposes quantum (semi–classical) dynamics on spin
configurations drawn from classical MC simulation, and so not subject to any
quantization condition.
And, crucially, the thermal distribution of the states generated by MC simulation
at low temperatures is conditioned by a classical, and not a quantum band
dispersion [cf.
Fig. 12
].

We can model the classical statistics found in MD simulation by “turning off”
the quantization of excitations in
Eq. (229)
,
and treating
x_{\nu,\textbf{k}}
and
p_{\nu,\textbf{k}}
as independent, classical, variables.
This will inevitably lead us back to the classical theory developed in
Section IV
,
but expressed in a form that makes it easier to draw conclusions about the relationship
between classical and quantum results.
Doing so, the partition function associated with
Eq. (229)
is given by

\displaystyle Z^{\sf CL^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=e^{-\beta(E_{0}+\Delta E_{0})}\prod_{\nu,\textbf{k}}\left[\left(\int{\rm d}x_{\nu,\textbf{k}}\ e^{-\frac{1}{2}\beta m\epsilon(\textbf{k})^{2}x_{\nu,\textbf{k}}^{2}}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\times\left.\left(\int{\rm d}p_{\nu,\textbf{k}}\ e^{-\frac{\beta}{2m}p_{\nu,\textbf{k}}^{2}}e^{\beta\sqrt{\frac{2\xi_{\sf S}^{2}(\textbf{k})}{m\hbar\epsilon(\textbf{k})}}\left(h^{z}_{\textbf{k}}\delta_{1,\nu}+h^{x}_{\textbf{k}}\delta_{2,\nu}\right)p_{\nu,\textbf{k}}}\right)\right]\;,

(230)

where
\beta=1/k_{B}T
.
The integrals in
Eq. (230)
can be evaluated exactly
[
Eq. (494a)
,
Eq. (494b)
], to give

\displaystyle Z^{\sf CL^{\prime}}=e^{-\beta(E_{0}+\Delta E_{0})}\prod_{\textbf{k}}^{N}

\displaystyle\left[\frac{2\pi}{\beta\epsilon(\textbf{k})}e^{\frac{\beta\xi_{\sf S}^{2}(\textbf{k})(h^{z}_{\textbf{k}})^{2}}{\hbar\epsilon(\textbf{k})}}\right.

\displaystyle\times\left.\frac{2\pi}{\beta\epsilon(\textbf{k})}e^{\frac{\beta\xi_{\sf S}^{2}(\textbf{k})(h^{x}_{\textbf{k}})^{2}}{\hbar\epsilon(\textbf{k})}}\right]\;.

(231)

By construction, this theory now describes excitations subject to the
classical (i.e. Boltzmann) statistics used in MC simulation.
We are now in a position to calculate equal–time spin correlations
using the same method as in
Section IV.3.2
, i.e. by
constructing a free energy and differentiating this with respect to
h^{\alpha}_{\textbf{k}}
.
Doing so, we find

\displaystyle S^{\sf CL^{\prime}}_{\rm{S}}(\textbf{q},T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha}{\langle\hat{S}^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q}}\hat{S}^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q}}\rangle}=\frac{4\xi_{\sf S}^{2}(\textbf{q})}{\beta\hbar\epsilon(\textbf{q})}\;,

(232)

where we have used the fact that
{\langle\hat{S}^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q}}\rangle}\equiv 0
in the FQ state.

(a)
\frac{\omega}{T}S^{\sf MD}_{\rm{S}}(\textbf{q},\omega),T=0.05

(b)
\frac{\omega}{T}S^{\sf MD}_{\rm{Q}}(\textbf{q},\omega),T=0.05

(c)
\frac{\omega}{T}S^{\sf MD}_{\rm{A}}(\textbf{q},\omega),T=0.05

(d)
S^{\sf QM}_{\rm{S}}(\textbf{q},\omega),T=0

(e)
S^{\sf QM}_{\rm{Q}}(\textbf{q},\omega),T=0

(f)
S^{\sf QM}_{\rm{A}}(\textbf{q},\omega),T=0

Comparison between dynamical structure factors found in molecular dynamics
(u3MD) simulations of a ferroquadrupolar (FQ) state, and those found in a
T=0

quantum theory.

Simulation results for dynamical structure factor associated with dipole
moments,
S^{\sf MD}_{\rm{S}}(\textbf{q},\omega)
, corrected for classical statistics,
following
Eq. (246)
.

Equivalent results for quadrupole moments,
S^{\sf MD}_{\rm{Q}}(\textbf{q},\omega)
.

Equivalent results for A–matrices,
S^{\sf MD}_{\rm{Q}}(\textbf{q},\omega)
.

Prediction for
S^{\sf QM}_{\rm{S}}(\textbf{q},\omega)
from
T=0
quantum theory
[
Eq. (193)
].

Equivalent prediction for
S^{\sf QM}_{\rm{Q}}(\textbf{q},\omega)
[
Eq. (201)
].

Equivalent prediction for
S^{\sf QM}_{\rm{A}}(\textbf{q},\omega)
[
Eq. (208)
].
Simulations were carried out using the u3MD simulation scheme described
in
Section III.2
, for
{\cal H}_{\sf BBQ}
[
Eq. (72)
] with
parameters
Eq. (212)
, at a temperature
T=0.05\ J
,
in a cluster of linear dimension
L=96
(
N=9216
spins).
All result have been convoluted with a Gaussian in frequency
of
FWHM = 0.35 J
.

The presence of the quantum dispersion
\epsilon(\textbf{q})
and
coherence factor
\xi_{\sf S}(\textbf{q})
in
Eq. (232)
,
is suggestive of the quantum theory developed in
Section V
.
And, by direct comparison with
Eq. (194)
,
we find

S^{\sf CL^{\prime}}_{\rm{S}}(\textbf{q},T)=2\frac{S^{\sf QM}_{\rm{S}}(\textbf{q},T=0)}{\beta\hbar\epsilon(\textbf{q})}\;,

(233)

a result which holds in the limit of low temperature.
At the same time,
S^{\sf CL^{\prime}}_{\rm{S}}(\textbf{q})
must ultimately be equivalent
to the earlier classical result
S^{\sf CL}_{\rm{S}}(\textbf{q})
[
Eq. (158)
].
To this end, we can simplify
Eq. (232)
using

Eq. (195)
, to recover

\displaystyle S^{\sf QM}_{\rm{S}}(\textbf{q})=2\xi_{\sf S}^{2}(\textbf{q})\;,

(234)

previously introduced as
Eq. (196)
.
It follows that

\displaystyle S^{\sf CL^{\prime}}_{\rm{S}}(\textbf{q},T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{\beta(A_{\textbf{q}}-B_{\textbf{q}})}=S^{\sf CL}_{\rm{S}}(\textbf{q},T)\;,

(235)

where this result also holds in the limit of low temperature.
Combining this with
Eq. (233)
, we arrive at
a result which relates classical correlations at finite temperature,
to those of a quantum system at
T=0
:

S^{\sf QM}_{\rm{S}}(\textbf{q},T=0)=\lim_{T\to 0}\frac{\hbar\epsilon(\textbf{q})}{2k_{B}T}S^{\sf CL}_{\rm{S}}(\textbf{q},T)\;.

(236)

The approach developed above can be generalised from dipole moments

\lambda={\sf S}
, to quadrupole moments,
\lambda={\sf Q}

and A–matrices,
\lambda={\sf A}
.
This leads to the general result

\displaystyle S^{\sf QM}_{\lambda}(\textbf{q},T=0)=\lim_{T\to 0}\frac{\hbar\epsilon(\textbf{q})}{2k_{B}T}S^{\sf CL}_{\lambda}(\textbf{q},T)\;,

(237)

where we make explicit the role of temperature,
and restore dimensional constants
k_{B}
and
\hbar
.
We emphasize that the factor of
\epsilon(\textbf{q})
in
Eq. (237)

reflects the dispersion for a
quantized
excitation [
Eq. (185)
],
and not the eigenvalue of a classical theory.
It is also important to note that quantum mechanics have been treated at
a semi–classical level, i.e. taking account of quantization, but considering
only one path in the path integral.
This approximation is, of course, exact for a SHO.

The principle problem encountered in “raw” MD results for dynamical structure
factors, relative to quantum results at low temperatures, was the loss of spectral
weight at low temperatures [cf.
Fig. 17
].
At low temperatures, we can equate
S^{\sf CL}_{\lambda}(\textbf{q},T)

with the structure factor found in MC simulation

\displaystyle\lim_{T\to 0}S^{\sf MC}_{\lambda}(\textbf{q},T)=\lim_{T\to 0}S^{\sf CL}_{\lambda}(\textbf{q},T)\;,

(238)

permitting us to write

\displaystyle S^{\sf QM}_{\lambda}(\textbf{q},T=0)=\lim_{T\to 0}\frac{\hbar\epsilon(\textbf{q})}{2k_{B}T}S^{\sf MC}_{\lambda}(\textbf{q},T)\;.

(239)

We can therefore use MC simulation to estimate the total spectral weight
in a zero–temperature quantum theory, at given
q
, as long as we had
prior knowledge of the characteristic energy scale
\epsilon(\textbf{q})
.
What remains is to understand the relationship between classical and quantum
results in the absence of prior knowledge of the dispersion.

The effect of MD simulation is to redistribute the spectral weight at
a given
q
over a range of different
\omega
, subject to the sum rule,

\displaystyle S^{\sf MC}_{\lambda}(\textbf{q},T)=\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}d\omega\ S^{\sf MD}_{\lambda}(\textbf{q},\omega,T)\;.

(240)

To estimate the zero–temperature quantum result

S^{\sf QM}_{\lambda}(\textbf{q},\omega,T=0)
, we therefore need to
construct a model for this redistribution of spectral weight,
subject to the condition that dynamics are treated at a semi–classical level.

Here it is instructive to return to the simulation results for fluctuations
about FQ order, described in
Section VI.4
.
From the “raw” results,
Fig. 16

and
Fig. 17
, we learn that

(i)

for
T\to 0
, the characteristic energy scale of excitations
converges on the exact quantum (semi–classical) result

\epsilon({\bf q})
[
Eq. (185)
], and

(ii)

excitations become sharp (resolution limited) for
T\to 0
.

FQ order, studied here, show a single, two–fold degenerate band of excitations.
More generally, there may be many different excitations at a given
{\bf q}
.
None the less, at a semi–classical level, (i.e. treated as a set of independent
oscillators), in a finite–size system, each of these will have a well–defined
energy.
It is also important to remember that, while only results for positive frequency
have been plotted in
Fig. 16
, MD simulation will return
solutions at both positive and negative energy, with equal weight
[
Eq. (110)
].

(a)

(b)

(c)

(d)

Figure 19:

Temperature dependence of results of
U(3)
Molecular Dynamics (u3MD)
simulation after correction for classical statistics, showing convergence
on the predictions of a
T=0
quantum theory for
T\to 0
.

u3MD results for dynamical structure
factor associated with A–matrices,
S_{\rm{A}}^{\sf MD}(\textbf{q},\omega)
, at wavevector

\textbf{q}=\textbf{K}
, for temperatures ranging from
T=0.01\ J
to
T=0.15\ J
.
Simulation results (points), have energy resolution
0.02\ J
,
and have been corrected for classical statistics through

Eq. (246)
.
Lines show fits to a Voigt profile,
Eq. (221)
.
The prediction of the
T=0
quantum theory,
Eq. (208)
,
convoluted with a Gaussian of
FWHM = 0.02 J
,
is shown with a solid red line.

Shift in peak energy
\Delta\omega(T)
,
found from fits to a Voigt profile, showing convergence of peak position
on the prediction of
T=0
quantum theory.

Equivalent results for the inverse lifetime
\Gamma(T)
.

Equivalent results for the peak height,
I(T)
.
Parameters for simulations are identical to those used in

Fig. 18
.

With these assumptions in mind, we model MD simulation results in the limit
T\to 0

in terms of delta–function peaks at energy
\omega=\pm\epsilon_{\nu}(\textbf{k})
,
with spectral weight shared equally between these two peaks, vis

\displaystyle\lim_{T\to 0}S^{\sf MD}_{\lambda}(\textbf{q},\omega,T)

(241)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu}\left[A_{\lambda,\nu}({\bf q},T)\delta(\omega-\epsilon_{\nu}(\textbf{k}))+A_{\lambda,\nu}({\bf q},T)\delta(\omega+\epsilon_{\nu}(\textbf{k}))\right]

\displaystyle\qquad+{\mathcal{O}}(T^{2})\;.

Here the sum on
\nu
runs over all eigenmodes of the cluster
with wavevector
{\bf q}
, and the corresponding spectral weight

\displaystyle A_{\lambda,\nu}({\bf q},T)=\frac{k_{B}T}{\hbar\epsilon_{\nu}({\bf q})}\xi_{\lambda,\nu}^{2}(\textbf{q})\;

(242)

is defined through a generalized coherence factor

\displaystyle\xi_{\lambda,\nu}^{2}(\textbf{q})\geq 0\;,

(243)

specific to the structure factor in question.
The total spectral weight in these modes is constrained
through
Eq. (240)
,
and satisfies

\displaystyle S^{\sf QM}_{\lambda}(\textbf{q},T=0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\omega\ S^{\sf QM}_{\lambda}(\textbf{q},\omega,T=0)

(244)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu}\xi_{\lambda,\nu}^{2}(\textbf{q})\;,

where the sum on
\nu
runs over the two degenerate branches of FQ excitations.
For the dipolar structure factor,
\lambda=S
, this sum contributes a
factor
\times 2
, and
Eq. (244)
can be compared
directly with
Eq. (196)
.

Where the model
Eq. (241)
holds, no prior knowledge of
excitation energies
\epsilon_{\nu}({\bf q})
is needed to correct for the
effect of classical statistics in MD simulation.
And since only positive frequencies, corresponding to transfer
of energy to the system, are relevant at
T=0
, we can write

\displaystyle S^{\sf QM}_{\lambda}(\textbf{q},\omega,T=0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{T\to 0}\frac{\hbar\omega}{k_{B}T}S^{\sf MD}_{\lambda}(\textbf{q},\omega,T)\;\;[\omega>0]\;.

Here
we understand that “
QM
” should be taken to imply
“semi–classical”, i.e. pertaining to excitations with quantum
statistics, treated a Gaussian level of approximation.
Empirical evidence for the validity of

is provided in
Section VII.2
, below.
Equivalent results for a system with many bands can be found in
in
Pohle et al. 2021
.

We conclude by noting that the approach of correcting for
classical statistics by multiplying dynamical structure factors by a prefactor
\omega/T
has been anticipated several times
in the literature of MD simulation, including in studies of the
spin–1/2 magnet Ca
10
Cr
7
O
28

Pohle et al. 2017
;
Pohle et al. 2021
,
the spin–1 magnet NaCaNi
7
O
7

Zhang et al. 2019
,
and dynamical scaling in Yb
2
Ti
2
O
7

Scheie et al. 2022
.
The factor
\omega/2T
used in
Pohle et al. 2021
reflects
a different normalisation of MD results.

VII.2
Quantum results, reconstructed

Armed with
, we are now in a
position to revisit MD simulation results for excitations about a FQ ground state,
previously discussed in
Section VI.4
.
In
Fig. 18
, we show a comparison
between MD simulation results, and the predictions of the zero–temperature
quantum theory developed in
Section V
.

Following
, simulation results
have been corrected by multiplying them by prefactor
\omega/T
, vis

\displaystyle\tilde{S}^{\sf MD}_{\lambda}(\textbf{q},\omega,T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\omega}{T}S^{\sf MD}_{\lambda}(\textbf{q},\omega,T)\;,

(246)

where the constants
k_{B}
and
\hbar
have again been set to unity.
Results obtained at
T=0.05\ J
, corrected in this way, are shown in
Fig. 18

–
.
In this case, u3MD simulations were carried out at a resolution
of
0.02\ J
, corrected according to
Eq. (246)
,
and then further convoluted with with a Gaussian envelope of

\text{FWHM}=0.33\ J
, so as to achieve a net energy resolution of

0.35\ J
, directly comparable to results in
Section VI.4
.

The results
in
Fig. 18

–
,
should be contrasted with the “raw” results
of MD simulation, shown
Fig. 16

–
.
Relative to these, corrected results show a far less spectral
weight at low energies, an effect which is particularly evident for

S^{\sf MD}(\textbf{q},\omega,T)
.
Meanwhile, for comparison, in
Fig. 18

–
, we reproduce equivalent
results from the
T=0
analytic theory, previously shown in

Fig. 12
.
Compared at the level of density plots, the agreement between corrected
simulation results
and the zero–temperature quantum prediction
is essentially perfect, with no visible mismatches in dispersion or intensity.

A more precise comparison between simulation and zero–temperature quantum
theory can be achieved by plotting
\tilde{S}^{\sf MD}_{\lambda}(\textbf{q},\omega,T)

at fixed wavevector
q
, for a sequence of temperatures converging on
T=0
.
This is accomplished in
Fig. 19

, where we plot
results for
\tilde{S}^{\sf MD}_{\sf A}(\textbf{q}=\textbf{K},\omega,T)
,
for temperatures ranging from
T=0.15\ J
to
T=0.01\ J
.
For comparison, we also show the result of the
T=0
analytic theory,

Eq. (208)
.
Both simulation and analytic prediction have been convoluted
with a Gaussian of FWHM
0.02\ J
.
Plotted in this way, the role of the limit in

becomes clear: MD simulation results corrected using

Eq. (246)
form a sequence
which converge on the
T=0
analytic prediction for
T\to 0
.

Having established the validity of
, it is
interesting to examine more precisely the way in which corrected
simulation results converge on the zero–temperature quantum result.
The dispersing peak in
\tilde{S}^{\sf MD}_{\lambda}(\textbf{q},\omega,T)

is
still
well–described by the Voigt lineshape,
Eq. (221)
, with fits
shown as solid lines in
Fig. 18

–
.
Within limits set by the energy resolution of simulations, these fits allow us to extract
quantitative estimates for the shift in excitation energy
\Delta\omega(T)
[
Fig. 19

],
the inverse lifetime of the excitation,
\Gamma(T)
[
Fig. 19

],
and the intensity maximum
I(T)
[
Fig. 19

],
as a function of temperature.

We find that the peak position converges linearly on the zero–temperature
quantum result from below, with

\displaystyle\Delta\omega(T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle bT+\mathcal{O}(T^{3})\;,\;\text{[b = 2.71]}\;,

(247)

Meanwhile, the inverse lifetime of the excitation vanishes (approximately) quadratically
as
T\to 0

\displaystyle\Gamma(T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle bT+cT^{2}+\mathcal{O}(T^{3})\;,\;\text{[b = 0.07, c = 3.26]}\;,

(248)

while the maximum intensity of the peak also converges linearly on
the expected value

\displaystyle I(T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a+bT+\mathcal{O}(T^{3})\;,\;\text{[a = 111, b = -1230]}\;,

(249)

where the coefficient
a
matches the prediction of the
T=0
quantum theory.

It is possible to construct a diagrammatic expansion for the self energy
of excitations within the mixed ensemble of MD simulation
Benton
.
Such calculations lie beyond the scope of this paper but, on general grounds,
it is possible to offer an interpretation of some of the trends observed in simulation.

At low temperatures, the shift in peak position,
\Delta\omega(T)

will depend on the density of excitations (one–loop diagram).
Because of the classical statistics of the MC simulation,
this density is linear in
T
.
Meanwhile, the inverse lifetime,
\Gamma(T)
, will be determined by interactions
which are present in finite–temperature simulations,
but absent from the Gaussian–level quantum theory developed
in
Section V
.
These processes correspond to Feynman diagrams with a finite
imaginary part, and will generically have the form of “bubbles”.
Empirically the dominant low–temperature contribution occurs at

{\mathcal{O}}(T^{2})
, i.e. at second order in the density of fluctuations.

We leave a more quantitative analysis of these effects for future work.

VII.3
Comparison with the results of QMC simulation

In
Section VII.2
, we have explored the correspondence
between u3MC simulation results at finite temperature, and analytic
quantum (semi–classical) results at
T=0
.
It is also interesting to consider how they compare with published
quantum Monte Carlo (QMC) simulation data.

VII.3.1
Ground state

The spin–1 BBQ model on a triangular lattice [
Eq. (1)
]
is accessible to QMC simulation for
J_{1}\leq 0
,
J_{2}\leq 0
.
(Equivalently, from
Eq. (5)
, the quadrant
-\pi\leq\theta\leq\pi/2
).
Stochastic series expansion (SSE) methods have been used to obtain results
for both thermodynamics and dynamics, at finite temperature, across this
parameter range
Völl and Wessel 2015
.
A more specialized loop–expansion method has also been used to study
properties at the special point
J_{1}=0
,
J_{2}=-1

(
\theta=-\pi/2
)

Kaul 2012
.

Both QMC results
Völl and Wessel 2015
, and u3MC simulations [
Section III.1.2
],
are consistent with a FQ ground state extending from
the special point
\theta=-\pi/2
, to the
SU(3)
point,
\theta=-3\pi/4
.
Meanwhile, for
-3\pi/4<\theta-\pi
, both methods find a FM ground state.
This distribution of FQ and FM ground states
is consistent with mean–field predictions [
Fig. 2
],
results from exact diagonalisation
Läuchli et al. 2006
, and more recent calculations
using tensor–network methods
Niesen and Corboz 2018
.

(a)
c(T)
as function of
\log T

(b)
\log c(T)
as function of
\log T

Figure 20:

Temperature–dependence of heat capacity found in Monte Carlo
simulations of the spin–1 bilinear–biquadratic model

{\cal H}_{\sf BBQ}
[
Eq. (1)
].

Heat capacity per spin,
c(T)
, plotted as a function of
\log T
.
Results are shown for simulations using quantum Monte Carlo (QMC),
classical Monte Carlo in space of
u(3)
matrices (u3MC), and
classical Monte Carlo in space of
O(3)
vectors (o3MC).
The peak in
c(T)
at intermediate temperatures reflects the onset of
fluctuations of ferroquadrupolar (FQ) order.

Equivalent results, plotted on a log–log scale.
At low temperatures, classical results tend to a constant reflecting
the number of generators of excitations about the ground state, while
quantum results show a power–law onset
c(T)\propto T^{2}
.
At high temperatures, all results scale as
c(T)=\alpha/T^{2}
.
Simulations were carried out for parameters
J_{1}=0.0
and
J_{2}=-1.0
,
with QMC data taken from
Völl and Wessel 2015
.

VII.3.2
Dynamics

Comparison between semi–classical simulations based on
u(3)
,
and QMC, is most straightforward for dynamics at low temperatures.
Here, as shown above, the u3MD approach exactly reproduces published results
from a (Gaussian–level) multiple–boson expansion
Läuchli et al. 2006
.
The comparison between QMC and the predictions of this
multiple–boson expansion is discussed in
Völl and Wessel 2015
.
At a qualitative level, good agreement is found between
the multiple–Boson expansion
at
T=0
, and QMC results for
T\ll J
.
It follows that agreement between QMC and u3MD simulations is
equally good, once the effect of classical statistics have been taken into
account [
Section VII.2
].

At a quantitative level, QMC results show some differences in values of
hydrodynamic parameters governing long–wavelength excitations, such as
the quadrupole–wave velocity
Völl and Wessel 2015
.
At low temperatures, the renormalisation of these parameters is a consequence
of quantum effects present in QMC, but not accessible within the semi–classical
description provided by Gaussian–level flavor–wave theory, or u3MD.
None the less, these quantum corrections are small, and become too small to
measure approaching the
SU(3)
point
\theta=-3\pi/4
.

Dynamics at temperatures
T\lesssim J
have also been explored using QMC
simulation
Völl and Wessel 2015
.
As temperature is increased, excitations near the top of the band, which have
predominantly spin–wave character, become heavily damped, and suffer a
dramatic loss of intensity [cf. results for
S_{\sf Q}(\textbf{q},\omega)

in
Völl and Wessel 2015
, Fig. 8].
Meanwhile quadrupolar fluctuations near the ordering vector show considerable
spectral weight at low energy.
The analysis of dynamics across the topological phase transition occurring
for
T^{*}\approx 0.4\ J
lies outside the scope of this paper.
However we note that similar trends in spectral weight are
observed in u3MD results for
S_{\sf Q}(\textbf{q},\omega)
, at temperatures
T\sim J
.
These will be discussed elsewhere
Pohle et al. in preparation.
.

For completeness, we note that a phenomenological theory
of the relaxational dynamics of spin–1 magnets has been introduced in

Bar’yakhtar et al. 2013
.
This makes the prediction that long–wavelength quadrupolar waves
have damping
\propto k^{2}
.
To the best of our knowledge, this phenomenological approach has yet to be
used to make quantitative predictions for FQ order in the BBQ model, or compared with
QMC simulation results.
We have made a preliminary analysis of damping as a function of
k
, within
u3MD simulation.
Precise evaluations of the damping of long–lived excitations at low energy
and temperature is challenging, but initial results are consistent with a damping

\displaystyle\Gamma(\textbf{k})

\displaystyle\propto

\displaystyle k^{2}+\mathcal{O}(k^{4})\;,\;

(250)

at fixed temperature
T/J\ll 1
.
We leave the further investigation of this point for future work.

VII.3.3
Thermodynamics

Probably the most interesting thermodynamic quantity to compare between
QMC and classical
U(3)
simulations is the heat capacity.
In
Fig. 20

we show results of simulations of the
spin–1 BBQ model [
Eq. (1)
] carried out
using QMC, classical MC in the space of A–matrices (u3MC),
and classical MC carried out in the space of
O(3)
vectors.
QMC simulation results are taken from
Völl and Wessel 2015
, while
u3MC results have already been introduced in
Section VI.1
.

O(3)
MC simulations parallel earlier work
Kawamura and Yamamoto 2007
.
Heat capacity per spin,
c(T)
, is shown plotted on both
log–linear [
Fig. 20

] and log–log scales
[
Fig. 20

],
with temperature measured in units of
J
.
We discuss the particulars of different temperature regimes below.

At low temperatures,
T/J\ll 1
, analytic theory for FQ order predicts

\displaystyle c(T\to 0)=2\times\frac{3\zeta(3)}{\pi v^{2}}T^{2}+\ldots

(251)

where
\zeta(3)\approx 1.2
,
v
is the velocity of the linearly–dispersing
Goldstone modes, and the factor
\times 2
comes from the fact these
are two–fold degenerate
Läuchli et al. 2006
.
This result follows from the Bosonic nature of low–lying excitations.
Fits to QMC simulation, confirm the expected
T^{2}
scaling
1
1

1

We note that interacting theory predicts a logarithmic correction to this scaling,

c(T)\sim T^{2}\ln T

Bar’yakhtar et al. 2013
, however published QMC results
may not extend to sufficiently low temperatures to distinguish this.
,
and return a value of
v
consistent with that found in simulations of dynamics
Völl and Wessel 2015
.

In contrast, classical MC simulations carried out in the basis of
u(3)
matrices
find
c(T\to 0)=2
[
Section IV.3
,
Section VI.1
].
The profound difference between classical and quantum results for
c(T\to 0)

is a consequence of the fact that, in the absence of quantum statistics,
entropy is not well posed for
T\to 0
.
And in this case, the effect of classical statistics cannot be corrected
as easily as for the semi–classical dynamics discussed above.

At intermediate temperatures,
T/J\sim 1
both classical and quantum
simulation results for
c(T)
are dominated by a large peak.
In all three cases, this peak is associated with the onset of fluctuations
of FQ order.
The peak found in classical simulations, which are carried out for
much larger systems, is sharp, and can be linked
to the unbinding of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
vortices
Kawamura and Yamamoto 2007
;
Pohle et al. in preparation.
.
Meanwhile, the peak found in QMC is much broader,
and occurs at a slightly higher temperature.
These differences reflect both different statistics, and the large
length scales needed to accurately describe a topological
phase transition.

Finally, we turn to the limit of high temperature,
T/J\gg 1
.
Here results must scale as

\displaystyle c(T\to\infty)=\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{\langle E^{2}\rangle-\langle E\rangle^{2}}{T^{2}}=\frac{\alpha}{T^{2}}\;,

(252)

where
\alpha
is coefficient depending on model parameters and the ensemble
of states sampled.
In
Fig. 20

this behaviour is reflected in parallel lines with gradient

\displaystyle\frac{d\ln c(T)}{d\ln T}=-2\;,

(253)

for
T/J\gtrsim 10
.
In this high–temperature limit, u3MC results
(
\alpha_{\sf u3MC}=0.16
)
are
intermediate between conventional
O(3)
MC simulations
(
\alpha_{\sf o3MC}=0.11
)
,
and QMC
(
\alpha_{\sf QMC}=3
)
.

It has been argued elsewhere that simulation in the
space of
{\bf d}
–vectors (vis A–matrices) should yield results equivalent
to QMC at high temperature
Stoudenmire et al. 2009
.
Empirically this is not the case.
And since, at high temperatures, finite–size effects are small, we infer
that the different values of
\alpha
found in different simulations
reflect different asymptotic values of the variance in energy,
Eq. (252)
.
This asymptote, and leading corrections to it, can be calculated within a
high–temperature series expansion
Oitmaa et al. 2006
.
We find this expansion takes on a different form for quantum spin–1
moments and A–matrices, and so will generally lead to different results

Shannon unpublished.
.
We leave further analysis of the high–temperature limit for future work.

VIII
Generalization to spin–anisotropic interactions

In the preceding sections of this paper, we have shown it is possible to calculate the
thermodynamic and dynamical properties of spin–1 magnets through simulations carried out
in the basis of
u(3)
.
So far, this analysis has been confined to the bilinear–biquadratic (BBQ) model,

Eq. (1)
, which is invariant under
SU(2)
spin rotations.
Here we show that the same approach can be applied to models with interactions
anisotropic in spin–space.

At first sight, this is not a trivial generalization, since the group
U(3)

encompasses spins with length
\neq 1
.
We therefore need to show that spin–anisotropic interactions do not
mix different spin sectors, at the level of individual spin–1 moments.
As we shall see, this condition is satisfied by both u3MC and u3MD simulations,
as long as dynamical simulations are initiated from a valid spin–1 state.

(a)
S^{\sf QM}_{\rm{S}}(\textbf{q},\omega),T=0

(b)
S^{\sf QM}_{\rm{Q}}(\textbf{q},\omega),T=0

(c)
S^{\sf QM}_{\rm{A}}(\textbf{q},\omega),T=0

(d)
\frac{\omega}{T}S^{\sf MD}_{\rm{S}}(\textbf{q},\omega),T/J=0.05

(e)
\frac{\omega}{T}S^{\sf MD}_{\rm{Q}}(\textbf{q},\omega),T/J=0.05

(f)
\frac{\omega}{T}S^{\sf MD}_{\rm{A}}(\textbf{q},\omega),T/J=0.05

Figure 21:

Dynamics of ferroquadrupolar (FQ) state in the spin–1 bilinear–biquadratic (BBQ)
model with easy–plane anisotropy.

Dynamical structure factor for dipole moments,
S^{\sf QM}_{\rm{S}}(\textbf{q},\omega)
,
as predicted by zero–temperature quantum theory of

Section VIII.2
.

Equivalent results for quadrupole moments,
S^{\sf QM}_{\rm{Q}}(\textbf{q},\omega)
.

Equivalent results for A–matrices,
S^{\sf QM}_{\rm{A}}(\textbf{q},\omega)
.

Dynamical structure factor
S^{\sf MD}_{\rm{S}}(\textbf{q},\omega)

found in molecular dynamics simulations within
u(3)
representation (u3MD) .

Equivalent results for
S^{\sf MD}_{\rm{Q}}(\textbf{q},\omega)
.

Equivalent results for
S^{\sf MD}_{\rm{A}}(\textbf{q},\omega)
.
Simulations were carried out for
{\cal H}_{\sf D}
[
Eq. (261)
],
with parameters
Eq. (268)
, at a
temperature
T=0.05\ J
, for a cluster of linear dimension

L=96
(
N=9216
spins).
Numerical results have been multiplied by a prefactor
\omega/T
to correct for classical statistics, following
Eq. (246)
.
All results have been convoluted with a Gaussian in frequency
of
FWHM = 0.35 J
.

VIII.1
Validity of u3MD approach

For simulations carried out in a
u(3)
basis to be valid, it must remain true
that each site in the lattice is host to a single spin–1 moment.
Once spins are transcribed in terms of generators of
U(3)
, this imposes
the condition that

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}\ \hat{\mathcal{A}}\equiv 1\;,

(254)

[
Eq. (10)
].
This condition is true by construction in u3MC simulation [
Section III.1
].
And, in
Section II.4
, we showed that

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t}\rm{Tr}\ {\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ }}\equiv 0\;,

(255)

for u3MD simulations carried out for the spin–rotationally invariant
BBQ model [
Eq. (1)
], implying that spin–length
is conserved.
We now extend this result to models which break spin–rotation invariance.

We consider most general form of spin–anisotropic Hamiltonian
allowed for a spin–1 magnet

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{\Delta}=\sum_{{\langle i,j\rangle}}{J^{\alpha\mu}_{\beta\nu}}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ \nu}^{\mu}}+\sum_{i}L^{\alpha}_{\beta}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}}\;,

(256)

where the only restriction placed on the interactions

J^{\alpha\mu}_{\beta\nu}
, and single–ion anisotropy
L^{\alpha}_{\beta}
,
is the requirement that
{\cal H}_{\Delta}
be Hermitian.
It follows that

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ \eta}^{\gamma}}=-i\left[\right.{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{{i}\penalty\ \eta}^{\gamma}},

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{\Delta}\left.\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{2}\sum_{\delta}\left(\right.

\displaystyle J^{\eta\alpha}_{\mu\beta}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{{i}\penalty\ \mu}^{\gamma}}+J^{\alpha\eta}_{\beta\mu}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{{i}\penalty\ \mu}^{\gamma}}

\displaystyle-J^{\mu\alpha}_{\gamma\beta}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{{i}\penalty\ \eta}^{\mu}}-J^{\alpha\mu}_{\beta\gamma}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{{i}\penalty\ \eta}^{\mu}}\left.\right){\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{{i+\delta}\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{2}\left(\right.L^{\eta}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{{i}\penalty\ \alpha}^{\gamma}}-L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{{i}\penalty\ \gamma}^{\alpha}}\left.\right)\;.

(257)

Setting
\eta=\gamma
and taking the trace, we find

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t}{\rm Tr}\ \mathcal{A}_{i}=-\frac{i}{2}\sum_{\delta}\left(\right.

\displaystyle J^{\alpha\gamma}_{\beta\mu}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{{i}\penalty\ \mu}^{\gamma}}-J^{\alpha\mu}_{\beta\gamma}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{{i}\penalty\ \gamma}^{\mu}}\left.\right){\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{{i+\delta}\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{2}\left(\right.L^{\gamma}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{{i}\penalty\ \alpha}^{\gamma}}-L^{\alpha}_{\gamma}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{{i}\penalty\ \gamma}^{\alpha}}\left.\right)\;,

(258)

where we have used the relationship

J^{\alpha\mu}_{\beta\nu}=J^{\mu\alpha}_{\nu\beta}\;,

(259)

which follows from the fact that components of
\mathcal{A}

on different lattice sites commute [
Eq. (8)
].
By rearranging indices on the right hand side of
Eq. (258)
,
we can easily show that

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t}\rm{Tr}\ {\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ }}=0\;,

(260)

as required.

It follows that the trace of
\mathcal{A}
is conserved within u3MD simulations,
and therefore that simulations carried for out for arbitrary spin–anisotropic interactions
respect the constraint on spin–length.
The implication of this result is that solving the
u(3)
equations of motion,

Eq. (73)
, for a spin–1 state, is exactly equivalent to solving
the much more complicated equations of motion for spin–1 moments
found in the algebra
su(3)

Balla 2014
;
Remund 2015
;
Zhang and Batista 2021
,
regardless of spin–anisotropy.

VIII.2
Application to FQ state with easy–plane anisotropy: analytic theory

For illustration, we now consider the simplest extension of the results developed
thus far to anisotropic interactions: the spin–1 BBQ model with single–ion,
easy–plane anisotropy

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{\sf D}={\cal H}_{\sf BBQ}+{\cal H}_{\sf SI}\;,

(261)

where
{\cal H}_{\sf BBQ}
is defined in
Eq. (1)
, and

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{\sf SI}=\sum_{i}D({\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{y}})^{2}\;,\quad[D>0]\;.

(262)

This model has previously been studied in
Onufrieva 1985
.

Like the BBQ model it descends from,
{\cal H}_{\sf D}
supports a FQ ground
state for a wide range of
(J_{1},J_{2}<0)
, and we can easily generalise
the theory of excitations developed in
Section V
to
take account of single–ion anisotropy.
Transcribing
{\cal H}_{\sf SI}
in terms of
\mathcal{A}
–matrices, by way of

Eq. (50)
, we find

\displaystyle{\cal H}_{\sf SI}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}D\left(-\frac{2}{3}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ y}^{y}}+\frac{1}{3}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ x}^{x}}+\frac{1}{3}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ z}^{z}}+\frac{2}{3}\right)\;.

(263)

Written in this form, it is immediately clear that
{\cal H}_{\sf SI}
is a special case of
the single–ion term in
Eq. (256)
.

From here, we can use
Eq. (173)
to express
{\cal H}_{\sf SI}
in terms
of the Bosonic basis introduced in
Section IV.1
.
Its effect is to introduce new diagonal terms in the matrix controlling
the dispersion of excitations [
Eq. (175)
], vis

\displaystyle M_{\textbf{k}}^{\sf{SI}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}A_{\textbf{k}}+D&-B_{\textbf{k}}&0&0\\
-B_{\textbf{k}}&A_{\textbf{k}}+D&0&0\\
0&0&A_{\textbf{k}}+D&-B_{\textbf{k}}\\
0&0&-B_{\textbf{k}}&A_{\textbf{k}}+D\\
\end{pmatrix}\;,

where
A_{\textbf{k}}
and
B_{\textbf{k}}
are given in
Eq. (128)
.
Solving the appropriate eigensystem [
Eq. (178)
],
we find two physical branches of excitation, with dispersion

\epsilon_{\textbf{k}}=\sqrt{(A_{\textbf{k}}+D)^{2}-B_{\textbf{k}}^{2}}\;.

(265)

It follows that the effect of easy–plane anisotropy is to open a gap

\Delta=\sqrt{2A_{0}D+D^{2}}\;,

(266)

to the Goldstone modes of FQ order.
This is to be expected since, in the presence of easy–plane anisotropy,
the FQ ground state does not break spin–rotation symmetry.

It is also straightforward to generalise the calculations of structure
factors described in
Section V.2
.
Results for
S^{\sf QM}_{\rm{A}}(\textbf{q},\omega)
[
Eq. (208)
],

S^{\sf QM}_{\rm{Q}}(\textbf{q},\omega)

Eq. (201)
] and

S^{\sf QM}_{\rm{S}}(\textbf{q},\omega)
[
Eq. (193)
]
can be adapted to easy–plane anisotropy through the simple substitution

\displaystyle A_{\textbf{k}}\longrightarrow A_{\textbf{k}}+D\;.

(267)

Doing so, and considering parameters

\displaystyle J_{1}=0\;,\;J_{2}=-1.0\;,\;D=0.2\;,

(268)

we obtain the predictions shown in

Fig. 21

–
.

VIII.3
Application to FQ state with easy–plane anisotropy: numerical results

Building on
Section VIII.1
, we can also apply the u3MD
simulation approach to the easy–plane model
{\cal H}_{\sf D}
[
Eq. (261)
].
In
Fig. 21

–
, we show
results obtained for the parameter set
Eq. (268)
.
Once corrected for the effect of classical statistics, through

Eq. (246)
,
simulations show good agreement with the predictions analytic theory
developed in
Section VIII.2
.

These results provide an explicit demonstration of the ability of u3MD simulations to
describe the excitations of spin–1 models with spin–anisotropic interactions.

IX
Summary, Conclusions and Outlook

In this Article, we have introduced a new method for simulating both the thermodynamics
and dynamics of spin–1 magnets, established the validity of this method
through detailed comparison with known limits, and used it to obtain a number of new
results for the spin–1 bilinear–biquadratic (BBQ) model on a triangular lattice.
Several other interesting findings entail.
Foremost among these is an explicit connection between classical simulations at finite
temperature, and zero–temperature quantum dynamics, treated at a semi–classical level.
Also of interest are a low–temperature expansion for the thermodynamic properties
of spin–1 magnets, and a novel derivation of a well–established multiple–Boson expansion.

The key to this method, introduced in
Section II
,
is the representation of spin–1 moments through the algebra
u(3)
. Unlike mappings onto an
O(3)
vector, this approach treats dipole and quadrupole
moments on an equal footing.
And for this reason, it provides a valid (semi–)classical limit of a spin–1 moment.
From this starting point, we have developed a framework for classical Monte Carlo
simulation in the space of “A–matrices”,
{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}}
, which act
as generators belonging to
u(3)
(u3MC).
We also derived equations of motion (EoM) for
{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}}
in a form suitable for
numerical integration [
Eq. (73)
].
These form the basis for a “molecular dynamics” scheme for exploring the dynamics of
spin–1 magnets (u3MD).

The numerical implementation of u3MC and u3MD simulations was described
in
Section III
.
A Marasaglia–like update in the space of
{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}}
was introduced,
and used to develop a MC scheme based on a local Metropolis update.
The resulting u3MC approach was shown to reproduce known results for the
thermodynamic properties of the spin–1 BBQ model on a triangular lattice,
and used to derive a finite–temperature phase diagram [
Fig. 3
].
Meanwhile, numerical integration of EoM using an RK–4 update was
shown to conserve the trace
{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}}
, establishing u3MD as valid approach
for simulating the dynamics of spin–1 magnets.

In order to illustrate the u3MC and u3MD approaches, we then turned to the
specific example of ferroquadrupolar (FQ) order, as found in the
spin–1

BBQ model on a triangular lattice [
Fig. 10
].
To better understand simulations, we first developed an analytic theory
of fluctuations about this state, described in
Section IV
.
Treated at a classical level, these fluctuations were shown to form bands with either
dipolar or quadrupolar character [
Fig. 12

–
],
which provide the framework for a classical low–temperature (low–T) expansion of the free energy [
Eq. (136)
].
This low–T expansion was used to make predictions for classical thermodynamic properties
of the BBQ model, for subsequent comparison with u3MC simulation.

Next, in
Section V
, we showed how these fluctuations
could be quantized, leading to a multiple–Boson expansion of excitations
about FQ order.
This theory, which is exactly equivalent to a known “flavor–wave” expansion,
was used to develop zero–temperature quantum predictions for
dynamical structure factors within a FQ state, for subsequent comparison
with u3MD simulation [
Fig. 12

–
].

With this ground work in place, in
Section VI
we explored both the
predictions of u3MC for the low–temperature thermodynamic properties of the FQ phase,
and the predictions of u3MD for its dynamics.
u3MC results for heat capacity
c(T\to 0)
[
Fig. 13
],
ordered moment
{\bf Q}
[
Fig. 14
]
and equal time structure factors
S_{\lambda}(\textbf{q})
[
Fig. 15
]
were shown to be in perfect agreement with the predictions of the classical
low–T
expansion.
Meanwhile “raw” u3MD results for dynamics were shown to give a good account of the
dispersion of excitations, but fail to reproduce their spectral
weight [
Fig. 16
].

The reason for the disagreement between u3MD and the
T=0
quantum theory
was identified as coming from classical statistics, inherited from u3MC simulation.
This observation formed the basis for a detailed exploration of quantum–classical
correspondence within u3MD simulation, building on the analytic theories of classical
and quantum excitations, and described in
Section VII
.
This analysis leads to a simple, and very general, prescription for correcting MD simulation
for the effect of classical statistics, in the limit
T\to 0
[
].
Corrected in this way, the predictions of u3MD were shown to perfectly reproduce the
predictions of zero–temperature quantum theory, considered at a semi–classical level
[
Fig. 18
and
Fig. 19
].
The comparison of u3MC and u3MD results with published QMC simulations of
the FQ phase of the BBQ model was also discussed.

Up to this point, all results were derived under the assumption of
SU(2)
symmetry,
appropriate to the BBQ model.
However many spin–1 magnets display anisotropy in their exchange interactions,
and at the level of individual ions.
For this reason, in
Section VIII
, we revisited the derivation
of the u3MD method, establishing that it remains valid for the most general
anisotropy permitted for a spin–1 magnet.
To illustrate this result, we demonstrated that u3MD simulations correctly
describe the dynamics of a FQ state in the presence of single–ion anisotropy,
perfectly reproducing the predictions of a
T=0
quantum theory
[
Fig. 21
].

We conclude that the u3MC and u3MD methods introduced in this Article provide
a reliable guide to the classical thermodynamics, and semi–classical dynamics
of spin–1 magnets.
This opens many new perspectives for both theory, and the interpretation of experiment.

On the theoretical side, the lack of well–developed methods means that the thermodynamic properties of spin–1 magnets, and in particular their phase transitions, remain relatively unexplored.
This is of particular interest for phases built of on–site quadrupole moments, which
cannot occur in spin–1/2 magnets, and for orders which support interesting topological
excitations.
Moreover the possibility of combining u3MC with u3MD means that, where an interesting
phase transition is identified, the associated dynamics can also be explored.

From this point of view, the phase diagram of the simple
BBQ model shown in
Fig. 3
already poses many interesting questions.
The ordered ground states of this model are already known to support a wide array of
topological excitations
Ivanov and Kolezhuk 2003
;
Ivanov and Khymyn 2007
;
Grover and Senthil 2011
;
Xu et al. 2012
;
Galkina et al. 2015
.
These take on particularly interesting form where the model has an
enlarged,
SU(3)
symmetry
Ivanov et al. 2008
;
Ueda et al. 2016
,
and the range of possibilities becomes still wider in the presence of
spin–anisotropy
Akagi et al. 2021a
;
Akagi et al. 2021b
;
Zhang et al. 2022
;
Amari et al. 2022
.
In the context of a two–dimensional model, this presents the opportunity
to study both the thermodynamics, and the dynamics, of a wide array
of different topological phase transitions.
We will return to this question elsewhere, in the context of FQ phase of
the BBQ model
Pohle et al. in preparation.
.

It would also be interesting to use u3MD to look more deeply into the
dynamics of spin–1 magnets at finite temperature.
While MD simulation does not respect quantum quantum
statistics, it does allow for interactions between quasiparticles.
Preliminary analysis of the damping of excitations, described in

Section VII
of this Article, suggest that u3MD results
are consistent with the predictions of hydrodynamic theories, at least at a qualitative level.
More work would be needed to put these results on a quantitative footing, but this
remains a promising avenue for future exploration.
To this end, it is worth noting that simulations based on
U(3)
are still in their infancy,
and there is considerable room for technical improvement, e.g., in Monte Carlo updates.

And very recently, there have been encouraging developments in the application of
SU(3)

approaches to spin–1 magnets, complimentary to the results of this Article
Dahlbom et al. 2022
;
Do et al. 2022
.

It is also interesting to speculate about the possible extension of a
U(N)
approach
to higher–spin moments.
Paradoxically, while increasing the size of the moment,
S
,
will suppress quantum fluctuations, it also increases the complexity to the
problem, through the number of parameters needed to describe a single site,
and the number of bands of excitations found on a lattice.
For example, the passage from spin–1 to spin–3/2 increases the Hilbert space from
to
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
to
\mathbb{CP}^{3}
, and the number of parameters needed
from
4
to
6
.
It also brings a new piece of physics, octupole moments at the level of a single site,
and a new algebra,
su(4)
.
This trend continues for larger
S
, with each moment possessing its own, unique,
semi–classical limit.
Taken appropriately, this limit should become an increasingly good approximation
as
S\to\infty
.

For this reason, further development of semi–classical methods for high–spin moments
makes very good sense.
To this end, we note that coherent–state representation has already been used to derive general
equations of motion for spin–
S
moments within the algrebra
su(N)
,
where
N=2S+1

Zhang and Batista 2021
.
Quite generally, it is possible to embed such an
su(N)
algebra within
u(N)
,
and seek simplification of the algebra representing the original spin–S moment,
of the type found for spin–1 in this paper.
We leave this as a topic for future study.

On the experimental side, many interesting spin–1 magnets have come to light.
Celebrated examples include the triangular–lattice spin–nematic
candidate NiGa
2
S
4

Nakatsuji et al. 2005
;
Nambu et al. 2006
;
Bhattacharjee et al. 2006
;
Tsunetsugu and Arikawa 2006
;
Läuchli et al. 2006
;
Valentine et al. 2020
,
and the pyrochlore spin–liquid candidate
NaCaNi
2
F
7

Plumb et al. 2019
;
Zhang et al. 2019
.
However there are also steady stream of new arrivals,
and interesting new results for older materials
Bai et al. 2021
.
Spin–1 models also arise in the context of cold atoms

Demler and Zhou 2002
;
Imambekov et al. 2003
;
Stamper-Kurn and Ueda 2013
;
de Forges de Parny et al. 2014
;
Zibold et al. 2016
,
and as a proxy for describing various forms of quantum liquid crystal, including
the nematic phases of Fe–based
superconductors
Fernandes et al. 2014
;
Luo et al. 2016
;
Wang et al. 2016
;
Gong et al. 2017
;
Lai et al. 2017
.
Many aspects of the physics of these systems remain ambiguous, and the ability
to simulate the dynamics of realistic, microscopic, spin–1 models could prove decisive.

The second major conclusion of this work, is that it is possible
to correct for the effect of classical statistics in finite–temperature
“molecular dynamics” (MD) simulations of magnets, and thereby use them to
study zero–temperature, quantum (semi–classical) dynamics.
This is a result of broad relevance, applying equally to conventional MD simulations
in the space of
O(3)
vectors.
And it goes some way to explaining why, despite its humble classical origins,
MD simulation has been so successful in describing the dynamics of exotic quantum
magnets
Taillefumier et al. 2017
;
Samarakoon et al. 2017
;
Pohle et al. 2021
.
The deeper exploration of this form of quantum–classical correspondence,
for example by pairing u3MD with QMC simulations, is another promising avenue
for future research.
And the
u(3)
basis which underpins this work could also be used as starting point
for explicitly quantum calculations, e.g. through the use of variational wave functions
based on tensor–networks.

Consider together, this is an absorbing set of problems, and it will be interesting
to see how much more can be learned through the numerical simulation
of spin–1 magnets.

Appendix A
Spin Fluctuation Probability

In this Appendix, we detail how the spin fluctuation probabilities drawn namely in
Fig. 1
,
Fig. 5
,
Fig. 10
,
Fig. 11
,
Fig. 22
, and
Fig. 23
are calculated.

Fluctuations around a given state
{\left|\alpha\right\rangle}
can be calculated by computing its spin fluctuation probability, defined as the spatial probability distribution of the overlapping between the state
{\left|\alpha\right\rangle}
and the spin coherent state
{\left|\Omega\right\rangle}
. The spin coherent state
{\left|\Omega\right\rangle}
is obtained by applying a rotation operator in 3 dimensions defined by the angles
\theta
and
\phi
on the m=1 state
{\left|1\right\rangle}
:

{\left|\Omega\right\rangle}=\mathcal{R}(\theta,\phi){\left|1\right\rangle}\;.

(269)

The spin coherent state represents then a spin pointing in the direction defined by the angles
\theta
and
\phi
.
In the case of a spin 1, the spin coherent state is expressed as:

{\left|\Omega\right\rangle}=\frac{1+\cos{\theta}}{2}e^{-i\phi}{\left|1\right\rangle}+\frac{\sin{\theta}}{\sqrt{2}}{\left|0\right\rangle}+\frac{1-\cos{\theta}}{2}e^{-i\phi}{\left|\overline{1}\right\rangle}\;.

(270)

The spin fluctuation probability of the state
{\left|\alpha\right\rangle}
is defined as the norm of the scalar product with the spin coherent state:

P(\theta,\phi)_{{\left|\alpha\right\rangle}}=\left|{\langle\alpha|\Omega\rangle}\right|^{2}\;.

(271)

Appendix B
Properties of A matrices

Here, we present the fundamental properties of the "A-matrix". From its definition in
Eq. (48)
, we note that the A-object is mathematically a (1,1)-tensor, but for simplicity, we might usually refer to it as a matrix. In this Appendix, we also give the detailed explanations accompanying the symmetry analysis of the BBQ Model [
Eq. (9)
] that we discuss at the end of
Section II
.

B.1
Properties of a single A matrix

First, we present how
Eq. (61)
is obtained.
Eq. (61)
tells us how an object like
{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}}
would transform under a general linear transformation
\Lambda
.
To this end, as explained in
Section II
, we consider a general linear transformation
\Lambda:V\rightarrow V
, such that
\textrm{det}\Lambda\neq 0
, so that
\Lambda
is invertible, and we define

\tilde{\Lambda}={\Lambda^{-1}}^{T}\;.

(272)

Under such a transformation, the basis vector
\textbf{e}_{i}
of the vector space
V
will transform according to

\overline{\textbf{e}}_{i}={\tilde{\Lambda}_{i}}^{\penalty\ j}\textbf{e}_{j}\;.

(273)

Since the vector
\textbf{v}=v^{i}\textbf{e}_{i}
is a mathematical object which existence does not depend on the basis, the components
v^{i}
should transform according to

\overline{v}^{i}={\Lambda}^{i}_{\penalty\ j}v^{j}\;,

(274)

such that the vector
\textbf{v}=\overline{v}^{i}\overline{\textbf{e}}_{i}=v^{i}\textbf{e}_{i}
stays invariant.
It is then also possible to introduce the dual basis
\{\textbf{e}^{\star i}\}
of the dual vector space
V^{\star}
. The basis vectors can be defined by the relations

{e}^{\star i}(e_{j})=\delta^{i}_{j}\;.

(275)

Any element
\textbf{v}^{\star}
of
V^{\star}
can be decomposed as

\textbf{v}^{\star}=v^{\star}_{i}\textbf{e}^{\star i}\;,

(276)

where the components
v^{\star}_{i}
are simply given by the value of the function
\textbf{v}^{\star}
on the basis vector
\textbf{e}_{i}
of
V

v^{\star}_{i}=\textbf{v}^{\star}(\textbf{e}_{i})\;.

(277)

Under a general transformation
\Lambda
on the basis vectors
\textbf{e}_{i}
, the dual basis vectors
\textbf{e}^{\star i}
will transform according to

\overline{\textbf{e}}^{\star i}=\Lambda^{i}_{\penalty\ j}\textbf{e}^{\star j}\;,

(278)

in order to preserve
Eq. (275)
.
And the component
v^{\star}_{i}
will transform as

\overline{v}^{\star}_{i}={\tilde{\Lambda}_{i}}^{\penalty\ j}v^{\star}_{j}\;.

(279)

Finally, under such a general transformation
\Lambda
, the component of an object like
{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}}
, which is actually a (1,1)-tensor, will transform as stated in
Eq. (61)
.

B.2
Properties of quadratric terms of A matrices

We here show how the products of two objects like
{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}}
would transform under a linear transformation, in order to analyze the symmetry properties of the BBQ Hamiltonian rewritten in terms of
{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\penalty\ }}
-“matrices” [
Eq. (9)
].
Again, we emphasis that an object like
{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}}
is mathematically a tensor, but for simplicity, we might sometimes refer to them as matrices.

Going back to
Eq. (9)
, it can easily be seen that the first term
{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ \alpha}^{\beta}}
is
U(3)
symmetric because both indexes
\alpha
and
\beta
are contravariant on one site and covariant on the other. The
{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ \alpha}^{\beta}}
will therefore stay invariant under a transformation
U\in U(3)
, for which we have

\begin{matrix}U\in U(3)\;:&UU^{\dagger}=U^{\dagger}U=\mathbb{I}&\penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ &U^{\dagger}=U^{-1}\;.\end{matrix}

(280)

Indeed, under a
U(3)
symmetry, the first term will transform as

\displaystyle({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}})^{\mu}_{\penalty\ \nu}({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ \alpha}^{\beta}})^{\nu}_{\penalty\ \mu}\rightarrow

\displaystyle U^{\mu}_{\penalty\ \gamma}{U^{\dagger}}_{\penalty\ \nu}^{\kappa}({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}})^{\gamma}_{\penalty\ \kappa}U^{\nu}_{\penalty\ \eta}{U^{\dagger}}_{\penalty\ \mu}^{\lambda}({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ \alpha}^{\beta}})^{\eta}_{\penalty\ \lambda}

\displaystyle=U^{\mu}_{\penalty\ \gamma}{U^{\dagger}}_{\penalty\ \mu}^{\lambda}U^{\nu}_{\penalty\ \eta}{U^{\dagger}}_{\penalty\ \nu}^{\kappa}({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}})^{\gamma}_{\penalty\ \kappa}({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ \alpha}^{\beta}})^{\eta}_{\penalty\ \lambda}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\gamma}^{\penalty\ \lambda}\delta_{\eta}^{\penalty\ \kappa}({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}})^{\gamma}_{\penalty\ \kappa}({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ \alpha}^{\beta}})^{\eta}_{\penalty\ \lambda}

\displaystyle=({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}})^{\gamma}_{\penalty\ \kappa}({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ \alpha}^{\beta}})^{\kappa}_{\penalty\ \gamma}\;.

(281)

The second term in
Eq. (9)
, on the other hand, is not
U(3)
symmetric, but it is
O(3)
symmetric. We can see that under a
U(3)
transformation, it transforms as:

\displaystyle({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}})^{\mu}_{\penalty\ \nu}({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}})^{\mu}_{\penalty\ \nu}\rightarrow

\displaystyle U^{\mu}_{\penalty\ \gamma}{U^{\dagger}}_{\penalty\ \nu}^{\kappa}({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}})^{\gamma}_{\penalty\ \kappa}U^{\mu}_{\penalty\ \eta}{U^{\dagger}}_{\penalty\ \nu}^{\lambda}({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}})^{\eta}_{\penalty\ \lambda}

\displaystyle=U^{\mu}_{\penalty\ \gamma}U^{\mu}_{\penalty\ \eta}{U^{\dagger}}_{\penalty\ \nu}^{\lambda}{U^{\dagger}}_{\penalty\ \nu}^{\kappa}({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}})^{\gamma}_{\penalty\ \kappa}({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}})^{\eta}_{\penalty\ \lambda}\;.

(282)

Clearly, this is not invariant under a
U(3)
transformation, but it is under a
O(3)
transformation. If
U=O\in O(3)
, we have

\begin{matrix}O\in O(3)\;:&OO^{T}=O^{T}O=\mathbb{I}&\penalty\ \Rightarrow\penalty\ &O^{T}=O^{-1}\;,\end{matrix}

(283)

and under a
O(3)
transformation, it transforms as:

\displaystyle({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}})^{\mu}_{\penalty\ \nu}({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}})^{\mu}_{\penalty\ \nu}\rightarrow

\displaystyle O^{\mu}_{\penalty\ \gamma}O^{\mu}_{\penalty\ \eta}{O^{T}}_{\penalty\ \nu}^{\lambda}{O^{T}}_{\penalty\ \nu}^{\kappa}({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}})^{\gamma}_{\penalty\ \kappa}({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}})^{\eta}_{\penalty\ \lambda}

\displaystyle={O^{T}}^{\penalty\ \mu}_{\gamma}O^{\mu}_{\penalty\ \eta}{O^{T}}_{\penalty\ \nu}^{\lambda}{O}_{\nu}^{\penalty\ \kappa}({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}})^{\gamma}_{\penalty\ \kappa}({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}})^{\eta}_{\penalty\ \lambda}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\gamma\eta}\delta^{\kappa\lambda}({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}})^{\gamma}_{\penalty\ \kappa}({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}})^{\eta}_{\penalty\ \lambda}

\displaystyle=({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}})^{\gamma}_{\penalty\ \kappa}({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}})^{\gamma}_{\penalty\ \kappa}\;.

(284)

The Hamiltonian is therefore overall
O(3)
symmetric, indeed both terms are invariant under an
O(3)
symmetry. And in the case of
J_{1}=J_{2}
, the second term in
Eq. (9)
vanishes, and the Hamiltonian is
U(3)
symmetric. Therefore, working in
U(3)
does not change the global symmetry of the Hamiltonian, since
o(3)\simeq su(2)
, and there is an homomorphism from
SU(2)
into
O(3)
. However, the locally augmented
SU(3)
symmetry of the Hamiltonian when
J_{1}=J_{2}
is enlarged from
SU(3)
to
U(3)
.

The Hamiltonian can be rewritten in a more general form as

{\cal H}_{\sf BBQ}=\sum_{{\langle i,j\rangle}}{J^{\alpha\mu}_{\beta\nu}}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ \nu}^{\mu}}\;,

(285)

with

J={\tiny\begin{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}J_{2}&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix}&\begin{pmatrix}0&J_{2}-J_{1}&0\\
J_{1}&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix}&\begin{pmatrix}0&0&J_{2}-J_{1}\\
0&0&0\\
J_{1}&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix}\\
\begin{pmatrix}0&J_{1}&0\\
J_{2}-J_{1}&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix}&\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&J_{2}&0\\
0&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix}&\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&0&J_{2}-J_{1}\\
0&J_{1}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}\\
\begin{pmatrix}0&0&J_{1}\\
0&0&0\\
J_{2}-J_{1}&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix}&\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&0&J_{1}\\
0&J_{2}-J_{1}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}&\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&J_{2}\\
\end{pmatrix}\\
\end{pmatrix}}\;.

(286)

The indexes
\alpha
and
\beta
correspond respectively to the line and the row of the table that assigns the designated matrix, whose components are then given by
\mu
and
\nu
. For example,

\begin{matrix}J^{1\mu}_{2\nu}=\begin{pmatrix}0&J_{2}-J_{1}&0\\
J_{1}&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix}&,\penalty\ &{J^{12}_{21}}=J_{1}&,\penalty\ &{J^{11}_{22}}=J_{2}-J_{1}\;.\end{matrix}

(287)

The symmetries of the Hamiltonian are now hidden in the symmetries of the tensor
J^{\alpha\mu}_{\beta\nu}
.
Firstly, we see that the Hamiltonian is
O(3)
symmetric, because the repeated summed indexes are always either covariant or contravariant.
The tensor is also symmetric under the exchange
{\alpha\beta}\leftrightarrow{\mu\nu}

J^{\alpha\mu}_{\beta\nu}=J^{\mu\alpha}_{\nu\beta}\;.

(288)

Eq. (288)
expresses the fact that there is actually a tensor product between the two operators
{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ \beta}^{\alpha}}
and
{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ \nu}^{\mu}}
acting on different sites.
We also have
{\alpha}\leftrightarrow{\nu}\penalty\ \textrm{with}\penalty\ {\beta}\leftrightarrow{\mu}
together, which is just relabeling the indexes.

In the case of
J_{1}=J_{2}
, the tensor is also symmetric under the exchanges
{\alpha}\leftrightarrow{\mu}
and
{\beta}\leftrightarrow{\nu}
or both

J^{\alpha\mu}_{\beta\nu}=J^{\mu\alpha}_{\beta\nu}=J^{\alpha\mu}_{\nu\beta}\;.

(289)

It is also symmetric under the exchanges

{\beta}\leftrightarrow{\mu}
and
{\alpha}\leftrightarrow{\nu}

J^{\alpha\mu}_{\beta\nu}=J^{\alpha\beta}_{\mu\nu}=J^{\nu\mu}_{\beta\alpha}=J^{\nu\beta}_{\mu\alpha}\;,

(290)

in which case, it can easily be seen that the Hamiltonian is
U(3)
invariant, since every index is now summed covariantly.

Appendix C
Conventions for the triangular lattice

In this Appendix, we present the convention that we used to describe the triangular lattice and its reciprocal space.
We choose the real space lattice vectors, linking a single site unit cell to another, to be

\begin{matrix}\textbf{a}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}\;;&\textbf{b}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
\sqrt{3}\end{pmatrix}\end{matrix}\;.

(291)

The associated vectors in reciprocal space are given by

\begin{matrix}\textbf{k}_{a}=\frac{2\pi}{\sqrt{3}}\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{3}\\
-1\end{pmatrix}\;;&\textbf{k}_{b}=\frac{2\pi}{\sqrt{3}}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
2\end{pmatrix}\end{matrix}\;.

(292)

We define the points along the irreducible wedge in reciprocal space to be

\begin{matrix}\bf{\Gamma}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix}\;;&\textbf{K}=\frac{4\pi}{3}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}\;;&\textbf{M}=\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{3}}\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{3}\\
1\end{pmatrix}\;.\end{matrix}

(293)

The vectors
\bf{\delta}
linking the 6 neighboring sites are given by

\begin{matrix}\bf{\delta}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\end{pmatrix}\;;&\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
\sqrt{3}\end{pmatrix}\;;&\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}-1\\
\sqrt{3}\end{pmatrix}\;;\\
\begin{pmatrix}-1\\
0\end{pmatrix}\;;&\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}-1\\
-\sqrt{3}\end{pmatrix}\;;&\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
-\sqrt{3}\end{pmatrix}\;.\end{matrix}

(294)

For the triangular lattice, the coordination number and the geometrical factor given in
Eq. (129)
yield

\begin{matrix}z=6&\;;&\gamma_{\triangleleft}(\textbf{k})=\frac{1}{3}(\cos(k_{x})+2\cos(\frac{k_{x}}{2})\cos(\frac{\sqrt{3}k_{y}}{2}))\end{matrix}\;.

(295)

The numerical simulations that we present in this Article are all performed on a cluster of sites defined by the real space basis vectors given by
Eq. (291)
and scaled by
L
, such that
N=L^{2}
is the number of lattice sites, with periodic boundary conditions.

Appendix D
Structure factors classically

We present here in more detail the results obtained in
Section IV.3
where we introduced a fictive field
h
that couples to the moments (dipoles, quadrupoles or A matrices) that we are considering [
Eq. (154)
].
This allows us to then take the appropriate derivatives of the free energy with respect to the fictive field components evaluated at zero-field, and calculate the desired thermodynamic quantities, such as the structures factors.

The calculation for the structure factors is divided into 2 parts. The first part is valid for
\textbf{q}\neq 0
. It consists in taking into account up to linear order in the expansion of fluctuations and is presented below.
We provide details of the calculation for
\textbf{q}\neq 0
for dipole moments in
Appendix
D.2
, quadrupole moments in
Appendix
D.4
, and A–matrices in
Appendix
D.6
.
The second part captures the ground state contribution at
\textbf{q}=0
and consists in taking into account up to quadratic order in the expansion of fluctuations. The general steps of the calculation at
\textbf{q}=0
are given in
Appendix
D.1
. The details at
\textbf{q}=0
are provided in
Appendix
D.3
for the dipole moments, in
Appendix
D.5
for the quadrupole moments, in
Appendix
D.7
for the A–matrices.

We assume that the field dependent part of the Hamiltonian is given by
Eq. (154)
,
and that the moment
\hat{O}^{\alpha}_{i,\beta}
can be written down in terms of the fluctuations
\phi_{i}
. Considering up to second order in fluctuations, the moments
\hat{O}^{\alpha}_{i,\beta}
becomes

\hat{O}^{\alpha}_{i,\beta}={q^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ \beta}}_{\mu\nu}\phi_{i}^{\mu}\phi_{i}^{\nu}+{l^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ \beta}}_{\mu}\phi_{i}^{\mu}+c^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ \beta}+\mathcal{O}(\phi^{3})\;,

(296)

where we implicitly sum over
\mu
and
\nu
, and
where
{q^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ \beta}}_{\mu\nu}
,
{l^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ \beta}}_{\mu}
and
c^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ \beta}
, are respectively the quadratic, linear, and constant coefficients from the expansion of
\hat{O}^{\alpha}_{i,\beta}
in terms ot the fluctuations
\phi_{i}
. The field depend part of the Hamiltonian then becomes

\Delta{\mathcal{H}}[{\bf{h}}_{i}]=-\sum_{i}{h}^{\alpha}_{i,\beta}{q^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ \beta}}_{\mu\nu}\phi_{i}^{\mu}\phi_{i}^{\nu}+{h}^{\alpha}_{i,\beta}{l^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ \beta}}_{\mu}\phi_{i}^{\mu}+{h}^{\alpha}_{i,\beta}c^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ \beta}\;,

(297)

where we also implicitly sum over
\alpha,\beta
, and where we neglect terms in
{\mathcal{O}}(\phi^{3})
, which will from now on be disregarded.
We now perform a Fourier transform according to
Eq. (126)
, and obtain

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{H}}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]=

\displaystyle-\sum_{\textbf{q}}\left[{l^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ \beta}}_{\mu}h^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q},\beta}\phi_{-\textbf{q}}^{\mu}+\sqrt{N}c^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ \beta}h^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q},\beta}\delta_{\textbf{q},0}\right]

\displaystyle-\sum_{\textbf{q}}\sum_{\textbf{k}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}{q^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ \beta}}_{\mu\nu}h^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q},\beta}\phi_{\textbf{k}}^{\mu}\phi_{-\textbf{q}-\textbf{k}}^{\nu}\;,

(298)

where
N
is the number of lattice sites.
We notice that if we were to include this in the total Hamiltonian
Eq. (153)
and write it down in the same form as
Eq. (124)
, the interaction matrix
M_{\textbf{k}}
would take the same dimension as the number of lattice site, because of the form of quadratic term in
Eq. (298)
. We should then calculate if for a fixed
q
. Namely for
\textbf{q}=0
, we get

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{H}}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]=

\displaystyle-{l^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ \beta}}_{\mu}h^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q}=0,\beta}\phi_{-\textbf{q}=0}^{\mu}-\sqrt{N}c^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ \beta}h^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q}=0,\beta}

\displaystyle-\sum_{\textbf{k}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}{q^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ \beta}}_{\mu\nu}h^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q}=0,\beta}\phi_{\textbf{k}}^{\mu}\phi_{-\textbf{k}}^{\nu}\;.

(299)

We note that the form of
Eq. (299)
is compatible with the form of
Eq. (124)
. Indeed, for
\textbf{q}=0
, the contribution of the second order in fluctuations will enter the interaction matrix
M_{\textbf{k}}
, modifying its eigenvalues, i.e., its relation dispersions, which will also depend on the field
h
, and the interaction matrix
M_{\textbf{k}}
can be easily diagonalized.
Therefore, we decide to only take into account up to second order in fluctuations for
\textbf{q}=0
, since it is exactly solvable and since we will need it when comparing the ordered moments at
\textbf{q}=0
, and to neglect them for
\textbf{q}\neq 0
. To make the fact that we are taking the second order in fluctuations into account only at
\textbf{q}=0
more obvious, we write

\displaystyle\Delta{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]=-\sum_{\textbf{q}}{l^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ \beta}}_{\mu}h^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q},\beta}\phi_{-\textbf{q}}^{\mu}

\displaystyle-\sum_{\textbf{q}}\left(\sqrt{N}c^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ \beta}h^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q},\beta}+\sum_{\textbf{k}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}{q^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ \beta}}_{\mu\nu}h^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q},\beta}\phi_{\textbf{k}}^{\mu}\phi_{-\textbf{k}}^{\nu}\right)\delta_{\textbf{q},0}\;.

(300)

We then rewrite the field depend part of the Hamiltonian such that the Hamiltonian is symmetric in
\vec{\phi_{\textbf{q}}}
and
\vec{\phi_{\textbf{q}}^{T}}
[
Eq. (131)
], which will be necessary when calculating the structure factors at
\textbf{q}=0
. We have

\Delta{\mathcal{H}}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]=-\sum_{\textbf{q}}\textbf{N}_{1}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]^{T}\vec{\phi}_{-\textbf{q}}+\vec{\phi}_{\textbf{q}}^{T}\textbf{N}_{2}[{\bf{h}}_{-\textbf{q}}]+\tilde{C}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]\delta_{\textbf{q},0}\;,

(301)

where we define

\begin{matrix}\rm{N}_{1}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]^{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}{l^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ \beta}}_{\mu}h^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q},\beta}\;,&&\rm{N}_{2}[{\bf{h}}_{-\textbf{q}}]^{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}{l^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ \beta}}_{\mu}h^{\alpha}_{-\textbf{q},\beta}\;,\end{matrix}

(302)

and

\displaystyle\tilde{C}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]

\displaystyle=\sqrt{N}c^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ \beta}h^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q},\beta}+\sum_{\textbf{k}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}q_{\mu\nu}^{\alpha}h^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q}=0,\beta}\phi_{\textbf{k}}^{\mu}\phi_{-\textbf{k}}^{\nu}\;.

(303)

\textbf{N}_{1,2}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]
are n–dimensional vectors whose components depend linearly on the fields
h^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q},\beta}
and represent the linear terms in
\phi_{\textbf{q}}
of the moments
\hat{O}_{\textbf{q}}
.
\tilde{C}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]
represents the 0–order term and the 2
nd
–order contribution in
\phi_{\textbf{q}}
of the moments
\hat{O}_{\textbf{q}}
at
\textbf{q}=0
.
\tilde{C}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]
is also linear in the fields
h^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q},\beta}
.
Plugging
Eq. (301)
in
Eq. (153)
, using the definition of the partition function in
Eq. (132)
, and using
Eq. (494e)
to perform the integral, we get

\displaystyle Z[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]

\displaystyle=e^{-\beta E_{0}}\prod_{\textbf{q}}^{N}\left[\sqrt{\frac{(2\pi)^{n}}{\beta^{n}\det(M_{\textbf{q}})}}e^{{2\beta}\textbf{N}_{1}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]^{T}M_{\textbf{q}}^{-1}\textbf{N}_{2}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]}\right.

\displaystyle\times\left.e^{\beta(\tilde{C}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]\delta_{\textbf{q},0})}\right]\;,

(304)

where
E_{0}
is given in
Eq. (125)
, and the
n\times n
square matrix
M_{\textbf{q}}
is given by
Eq. (127)
.
n
is the dimension of
M_{\textbf{q}}
, i.e., the number of independent classical fluctuations. In our case, we have
n=4
.
N
is the number of lattice sites.

The free energy then becomes

\displaystyle F[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]=

\displaystyle-\frac{\log(Z[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}])}{\beta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{0}-\sum_{\textbf{q}}^{N}\tilde{C}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]\delta_{\textbf{q},0}-2\sum_{\textbf{q}}^{N}\textbf{N}_{1}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]^{T}M_{\textbf{q}}^{-1}\textbf{N}_{2}[{\bf{h}}_{-\textbf{q}}]

\displaystyle+\frac{n}{2\beta}\sum_{\textbf{q}}\log(\frac{\beta}{2\pi})+\frac{1}{2\beta}\sum_{\textbf{q}}\log({\det}({M}_{\textbf{q}}))

\displaystyle+{\mathcal{O}}(T^{2})\;.

(305)

The first derivatives of the free energy with respect to field components
{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}
give

{\langle\hat{O}^{\mu}_{\textbf{q},\nu}\rangle}=-\left.\frac{{\partial}F}{{\partial}h^{\mu}_{\textbf{q},\nu}}\right|_{\textbf{h}=0}=\left.\frac{{\partial}\tilde{C}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]}{{\partial}h^{\mu}_{\textbf{q},\nu}}\right|_{\textbf{h}=0}\delta_{\textbf{q},0}\;.

(306)

The second derivatives of the free energy with respect to field components
{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}
correspond to

\displaystyle{\langle\hat{O}^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q},\beta}\hat{O}^{\mu}_{-\textbf{q},\nu}\rangle}-{\langle\hat{O}^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q},\beta}\rangle}{\langle\hat{O}^{\mu}_{-\textbf{q},\nu}\rangle}=-\left.\frac{1}{\beta}\frac{{\partial}^{2}F}{{\partial}h^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q},\beta}{\partial}h^{\mu}_{-\textbf{q},\nu}}\right|_{\textbf{h}=0}

(307a)

\displaystyle=\left.\frac{2}{\beta}\frac{{\partial}^{2}}{{\partial}h^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q},\beta}{\partial}h^{\mu}_{-\textbf{q},\nu}}\sum_{\textbf{q}}^{N}(\textbf{N}_{1}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]^{T}M_{\textbf{q}}^{-1}\textbf{N}_{2}[{\bf{h}}_{-\textbf{q}}])\right|_{\textbf{h}=0}\;,

(307b)

where we used the fact that
\tilde{C}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]
is linear in the field components
h^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q},\beta}
.
For
\textbf{q}\neq 0
, it turns out to be more convenient to work with
\tilde{M_{\textbf{q}}}
[
Eq. (144)
] which is diagonal and which inverse then simply holds

(\tilde{M_{\textbf{q}}}^{-1})^{\lambda\lambda}=\frac{1}{\tilde{M_{\textbf{q}}}^{\lambda\lambda}}=\frac{1}{\omega_{\textbf{q},\lambda}}\;.

(308)

We are allowed to do this because for
\textbf{q}\neq 0
, the interaction matrix stays unchanged. However, we need to be more careful for
\textbf{q}=0
as explained in
Appendix
D.1
.
Then,
\textbf{N}_{1,2}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]
become
\tilde{\textbf{N}}_{1,2}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]

\displaystyle\tilde{\textbf{N}}_{1}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]^{T}

\displaystyle=\textbf{N}_{1}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]^{T}O\;,

(309a)

\displaystyle\tilde{\textbf{N}}_{2}[{\bf{h}}_{-\textbf{q}}]

\displaystyle=O^{T}\textbf{N}_{2}[{\bf{h}}_{-\textbf{q}}]\;,

(309b)

such that
\tilde{\textbf{N}}_{1,2}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]
corresponds to the linear term when expressing the operators
\hat{O}^{\alpha}_{i}
in terms of the fluctuations
\vec{v}_{\textbf{q}}
that diagonalize the BBQ Hamiltonian as shown in
Eq. (142)
.
Indeed, we then obtain

\Delta{\mathcal{H}}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]=-\sum_{\textbf{q}}\tilde{\textbf{N}}_{1}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]^{T}\vec{v}_{-\textbf{q}}+\vec{v}_{\textbf{q}}^{T}\tilde{\textbf{N}}_{2}[{\bf{h}}_{-\textbf{q}}]+\tilde{C}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]\delta_{\textbf{q},0}\;,

(310)

Therefore, we can simply write

\displaystyle F[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]=

\displaystyle E_{0}+\sum_{\textbf{q}}^{N}\tilde{C}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]\delta_{\textbf{q},0}-2\sum_{\textbf{q}}^{N}\sum_{\lambda=1}^{N_{\lambda}}\frac{(\tilde{\textbf{N}}_{1}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]^{T})^{\lambda}\tilde{\textbf{N}}_{2}[{\bf{h}}_{-\textbf{q}}]^{\lambda}}{\omega_{\textbf{q},\lambda}}

\displaystyle+\frac{N_{\lambda}}{2\beta}\sum_{\textbf{q}}\log(\frac{\beta}{2\pi})+\frac{1}{2\beta}\sum_{\textbf{q}}\sum_{\lambda=1}^{N_{\lambda}}\log(\omega_{\textbf{q},\lambda})+{\mathcal{O}}(T^{2})\;,

(311)

where we have used
Eq. (137)
and where
N_{\lambda}=4
is the number of modes.

Eq. (306)
stays unchanged, but
Eq. (307b)
takes the simple form given by

\displaystyle{\langle\hat{O}^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q},\beta}\hat{O}^{\mu}_{-\textbf{q},\nu}\rangle}-{\langle\hat{O}^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q},\beta}\rangle}{\langle\hat{O}^{\mu}_{-\textbf{q},\nu}\rangle}=

(312)
