\displaystyle=-\left.\frac{{\partial}\left[\Delta E_{0}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]+C[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]\right]}{{\partial}h^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q}=0,\beta}}\right|_{\textbf{h}=0}

\displaystyle+2\left.\frac{{\partial}\left[\frac{n_{1}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{k}=0}]n_{2}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{k}=0}]}{\epsilon_{\textbf{k},\alpha}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]}+\frac{n_{3}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{k}=0}]n_{4}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{k}=0}]}{\epsilon_{\textbf{k},\alpha}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]}\right]}{{\partial}h^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q}=0,\beta}}\right|_{\textbf{h}=0}\;.

(457)

We also note that the terms with
n_{1}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{k}=0}]n_{2}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{k}=0}]
and
n_{3}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{k}=0}]n_{4}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{k}=0}]
are at least quadratic (if not of higher order, depending on
\epsilon_{\textbf{k}}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]
) in the field components
{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}
, as any of the
n_{i=1,2,3,4}
is independently linear in
{\bf{h}}_{-\textbf{k}=0}
by definition. Therefore taking the first derivative of the terms with
n_{1}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{k}=0}]n_{2}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{k}=0}]
and
n_{3}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{k}=0}]n_{4}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{k}=0}]
and evaluating them at zero field will inevitably lead to a null contribution. We then are simply left with

\displaystyle{\langle\hat{O}^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q}=0,\beta}\rangle}_{T=0}

\displaystyle=-\left.\frac{{\partial}\Delta E_{0}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]+C[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]}{{\partial}h^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q}=0,\beta}}\right|_{\textbf{h}=0}\;.

(458)

The second moments are given by
Eq. (323)
and disregarding again the term with
\frac{1}{\beta}
, it simply becomes

\displaystyle{\langle\hat{O}^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q}=0,\beta}\hat{O}^{\mu}_{\textbf{q}=0,\nu}\rangle}_{T=0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\langle\hat{O}^{\alpha}_{\textbf{q}=0,\beta}\rangle}_{T=0}{\langle\hat{O}^{\mu}_{\textbf{q}=0,\nu}\rangle}_{T=0}\;.

We can insert
Section F.5
into
Eq. (441)
to calculate the ground state contribution to the quantum structure factor.
Therefore all we need to do is find the 0
th
order contribution of the source term, i.e., find
C[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]
, and compute the zero–point energy of the Bogoliubov transformation
\Delta E_{0}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]
.

F.6
Dipole moments: contribution of the ground state to the quantum structure factor at
\textbf{q}=0

First, we consider the structure factor for dipole moments of spin

\displaystyle S^{\sf GS}_{\rm{S}}(\textbf{q}=0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha}{\langle{\hat{\rm{S}}_{\textbf{q}=0}^{\alpha}}{\hat{\rm{S}}_{-\textbf{q}=0}^{\alpha}}\rangle}_{T=0}\;.

(460)

The relevant source term is given by
Eq. (328)

According to
Eq. (50)
and using
Eq. (173)
, we can express
Eq. (328)
in function of fluctuations orthogonal to the FQ ground state [
Eq. (112)
].
Considering fluctuation terms up to 2
nd
order, we have

\displaystyle{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{x}}

\displaystyle=-i({\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}}-{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}^{\dagger}})\;,

\displaystyle{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{y}}

\displaystyle=i({\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}^{\dagger}}{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}}-{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}}{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}^{\dagger}})\;,

(461)

\displaystyle{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{z}}

\displaystyle=-i({\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}^{\dagger}}-{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}})\;.

After performing a Fourier transform, and considering the source term Hamiltonian
\Delta{\cal H}[{h}^{\alpha}_{i,\beta}]
[
Eq. (328)
] at
\textbf{q}=0
, we have

\displaystyle\Delta{\cal H}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}}]=-\sum_{\textbf{q}}

\displaystyle\left[ih^{x}_{-\textbf{q}}({\hat{\rm{b}}_{\textbf{q}}^{\dagger}}-{\hat{\rm{b}}_{\textbf{q}}})-ih^{z}_{-\textbf{q}}({\hat{\rm{a}}_{\textbf{q}}^{\dagger}}-{\hat{\rm{a}}_{\textbf{q}}})\right]\delta_{\textbf{q},0}

\displaystyle-\sum_{\textbf{k}}

\displaystyle\frac{i}{\sqrt{N}}h^{y}_{\textbf{q}=0}({\hat{\rm{a}}_{\textbf{k}}^{\dagger}}{\hat{\rm{b}}_{\textbf{k}}}-{\hat{\rm{a}}_{\textbf{k}}}{\hat{\rm{b}}_{\textbf{k}}^{\dagger}})\;.

(462)

And using
Eq. (174)
for the BBQ Hamiltonian, the total Hamiltonian [
Eq. (153)
] in terms of the bosons takes the same form as in
Eq. (443)
,
where
{M}_{\textbf{k}}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]
is given by

\displaystyle M_{\textbf{k}}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}A_{\textbf{k}}&-B_{\textbf{k}}&-\frac{i}{\sqrt{N}}h^{y}_{\textbf{q}=0}&0\\
-B_{\textbf{k}}&A_{\textbf{k}}&0&\frac{i}{\sqrt{N}}h^{y}_{\textbf{q}=0}\\
\frac{i}{\sqrt{N}}h^{y}_{\textbf{q}=0}&0&A_{\textbf{k}}&-B_{\textbf{k}}\\
0&-\frac{i}{\sqrt{N}}h^{y}_{\textbf{q}=0}&-B_{\textbf{k}}&A_{\textbf{k}}\\
\end{pmatrix}\;,

(463a)

where
A_{\textbf{k}}
and
B_{\textbf{k}}
are given in
Eq. (128)

and where
{\textbf{N}}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{k}}]
is given by

\begin{matrix}{\textbf{N}}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{k}}]=i\begin{pmatrix}h^{z}_{-\textbf{k}}\\
-h^{z}_{\textbf{k}}\\
-h^{x}_{-\textbf{k}}\\
h^{x}_{\textbf{k}}\end{pmatrix}\;,\end{matrix}

(463b)

and where
C[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]
holds

C[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]=0\;.

(463c)

Following the procedure depicted in
Section V
and detailed in
Appendix
E
, we perform a Bogoliubov transformation and
the eigenvalues
\epsilon_{\textbf{k},\lambda}
are given by

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\textbf{k},1}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]

\displaystyle=-\epsilon_{\textbf{k},2}=+\sqrt{A_{\textbf{k}}^{2}-B_{\textbf{k}}^{2}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}h^{y}_{\textbf{q}=0}\;,

(464a)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\textbf{k},3}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]

\displaystyle=-\epsilon_{\textbf{k},4}=+\sqrt{A_{\textbf{k}}^{2}-B_{\textbf{k}}^{2}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}h^{y}_{\textbf{q}=0}\;.

(464b)

After performing the Bogoliubov transformation, the Hamiltonian can be rewritten as follows:

\displaystyle{\cal H}=

\displaystyle E_{0}+\Delta E_{0}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]

\displaystyle+\left[\sum_{\textbf{k}}\epsilon_{\textbf{k},1}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]{\hat{\alpha}_{\textbf{k}}^{\dagger}}{\hat{\alpha}_{\textbf{k}}}+\epsilon_{\textbf{k},3}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]{\hat{\beta}_{\textbf{k}}^{\dagger}}{\hat{\beta}_{\textbf{k}}}\right]\;,

(465)

where
C[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]
is disregarded since it is null [
Eq. (463c)
], and where
\Delta E_{0}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]
is the zero–point energy

\displaystyle\Delta E_{0}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\textbf{k}}\left[\epsilon_{\textbf{k},1}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]+\epsilon_{\textbf{k},3}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]\right]\;.

(466)

According to
Eq. (458)
, the ground state contribution to the first moments yield

\displaystyle{\langle S^{x}_{\textbf{q}}\rangle}_{T=0}

\displaystyle={\langle S^{z}_{\textbf{q}}\rangle}_{T=0}=0\;,

(467a)

\displaystyle{\langle S^{y}_{\textbf{q}}\rangle}_{T=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\textbf{k}}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\right]=0\;.

(467b)

And according to
Section F.5
and
Eq. (460)
, the spin dipole structure factor at
\textbf{q}=0
yields

\displaystyle S^{\sf GS}_{\rm{S}}(\textbf{q}=0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;.

(468)

Indeed, the ground state is quadrupolar and does not break time–reversal symmetry. Therefore, at zero temperature, the contribution of quantum fluctuations from the zero–point energy should average to zero for the spin dipole moments.
The spectral representation of
Eq. (468)
is then also trivially null.
Combining
Eq. (430)
and
Eq. (468)
, we obtain
Eq. (193)
.

F.7
Quadrupole moments: contribution of the ground state to the quantum structure factor at
\textbf{q}=0

We now consider the quadrupole structure factor at the
\Gamma
–point, which is defined as

\displaystyle S^{\sf GS}_{\rm{Q}}(\textbf{q}=0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\beta}{\langle{\hat{\rm{Q}}_{\textbf{q}=0}^{\alpha\beta}}{\hat{\rm{Q}}_{\textbf{q}=0}^{\beta\alpha}}\rangle}_{T=0}\;.

(469)

We follow the same procedure as depicted in
Appendix
F.5
. The relevant source term for quadrupole moments is given by
Eq. (345)
. We can express
Eq. (345)
up to second order in terms of the bosons by using
Eq. (173)
and
Eq. (51)
. We use
Eq. (174)
for the BBQ Hamiltonian. We then obtain for the total Hamiltonian given by
Eq. (153)
, written in the form of
Eq. (443)
, where

M_{\textbf{k}}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]
is given by

M_{\textbf{k}}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]=\begin{pmatrix}A_{\textbf{k}}+\alpha_{1}&-B_{\textbf{k}}&\beta_{1}&0\\
-B_{\textbf{k}}&A_{\textbf{k}}+\alpha_{1}&0&\beta_{1}\\
\beta_{1}&0&A_{\textbf{k}}+\alpha_{2}&-B_{\textbf{k}}\\
\beta_{1}&0&-B_{\textbf{k}}&A_{\textbf{k}}+\alpha_{2}\\
\end{pmatrix}\;,

(470a)

where
{\textbf{N}}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{k}}]
is given by

\begin{matrix}{\textbf{N}}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{k}}]=\begin{pmatrix}\xi^{1}_{-\textbf{k}}\\
\xi^{1}_{\textbf{k}}\\
\xi^{2}_{-\textbf{k}}\\
\xi^{2}_{-\textbf{k}}\end{pmatrix}\end{matrix}\;,

(470b)

and where
C[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]
holds

C[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]=\sqrt{N}\left(\frac{4}{3}h_{\textbf{q}=0,y}^{y}-\frac{2}{3}(h_{\textbf{q}=0,x}^{x}+h_{\textbf{q}=0,z}^{z})\right)\;,

(470c)

with
A_{\textbf{k}}
and
B_{\textbf{k}}
being given in
Eq. (128)
and with the following definitions

\begin{matrix}\begin{matrix}\alpha_{1}=\frac{2}{\sqrt{N}}(h_{\textbf{q}=0,x}^{x}-h_{\textbf{q}=0,y}^{y})\;,&\alpha_{2}=\frac{2}{\sqrt{N}}(h_{\textbf{q}=0,z}^{z}-h_{\textbf{q}=0,y}^{y})\;,\end{matrix}\\
&\\
\begin{matrix}\beta_{1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}(h_{\textbf{q}=0,z}^{x}+h_{\textbf{q}=0,x}^{z})\;,\end{matrix}\\
&\\
\begin{matrix}\xi^{1}_{\textbf{k}}=(h^{x}_{\textbf{k},y}+h^{y}_{\textbf{k},x})&\;,&\xi^{2}_{\textbf{k}}=(h^{y}_{\textbf{k},z}+h^{z}_{\textbf{k},y})\end{matrix}\;.\end{matrix}

(471)

Following the procedure depicted in
Section V
and detailed in
Appendix
E
, we perform a Bogoliubov transformation and
the eigenvalues
\epsilon_{\textbf{k},\lambda}
are given by

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\textbf{k},1}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]=-\epsilon_{\textbf{k},2}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]=

\displaystyle\sqrt{A_{\textbf{k}}^{2}-B_{\textbf{k}}^{2}+\beta_{1}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha_{1}^{2}+\alpha_{2}^{2}\right)+A_{\textbf{k}}\alpha^{+}-\Delta\left(A_{\textbf{k}}+\frac{\alpha^{+}}{2}\right)}\;,

(472a)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\textbf{k},3}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]=-\epsilon_{\textbf{k},4}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]=

\displaystyle\sqrt{A_{\textbf{k}}^{2}-B_{\textbf{k}}^{2}+\beta_{1}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\alpha_{1}^{2}+\alpha_{2}^{2}\right)+A_{\textbf{k}}\alpha^{+}+\Delta\left(A_{\textbf{k}}+\frac{\alpha^{+}}{2}\right)}\;,

(472b)

where
\alpha^{+}
and
\Delta
are defined in
Eq. (357)
.
After performing the Bogoliubov transformation, the Hamiltonian can be rewritten as follows:

\displaystyle{\cal H}=

\displaystyle E_{0}+\Delta E_{0}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]+C[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]

\displaystyle+\left[\sum_{\textbf{k}}\epsilon_{\textbf{k},1}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]{\hat{\alpha}_{\textbf{k}}^{\dagger}}{\hat{\alpha}_{\textbf{k}}}+\epsilon_{\textbf{k},3}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]{\hat{\beta}_{\textbf{k}}^{\dagger}}{\hat{\beta}_{\textbf{k}}}\right]\;,

(473)

where
C[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]
is given in
Eq. (470c)
, and where
\Delta E_{0}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]
is the zero–point energy and yields

\displaystyle\Delta E_{0}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\textbf{k}}\left[\epsilon_{\textbf{k},1}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]+\epsilon_{\textbf{k},3}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]\right]\;.

(474)

According to
Eq. (458)
, the ground state contribution to the first moments yield

\displaystyle{\langle Q^{xx}_{\textbf{q}=0}\rangle}_{T=0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{3}\sqrt{N}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{\textbf{k}}\left[\frac{A_{\textbf{k}}}{\sqrt{A_{\textbf{k}}^{2}-B_{\textbf{k}}^{2}}}\right]\;,

(475a)

\displaystyle{\langle Q^{xy}_{\textbf{q}=0,y}\rangle}_{T=0}

\displaystyle={\langle Q^{yx}_{\textbf{q}=0}\rangle}_{T=0}=0\;,

(475b)

\displaystyle{\langle Q^{xz}_{\textbf{q}=0,z}\rangle}_{T=0}

\displaystyle={\langle Q^{zx}_{\textbf{q}=0}\rangle}_{T=0}=0\;,

(475c)

\displaystyle{\langle Q^{yy}_{\textbf{q}=0,y}\rangle}_{T=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{4}{3}\sqrt{N}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{\textbf{k}}\left[\frac{2A_{\textbf{k}}}{\sqrt{A_{\textbf{k}}^{2}-B_{\textbf{k}}^{2}}}\right]\;,

(475d)

\displaystyle{\langle Q^{yz}_{\textbf{q}=0,z}\rangle}_{T=0}

\displaystyle={\langle Q^{zy}_{\textbf{q}=0}\rangle}_{T=0}=0\;,

(475e)

\displaystyle{\langle Q^{zz}_{\textbf{q}=0}\rangle}_{T=0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{3}\sqrt{N}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{\textbf{k}}\left[\frac{A_{\textbf{k}}}{\sqrt{A_{\textbf{k}}^{2}-B_{\textbf{k}}^{2}}}\right]\;.

(475f)

Before calculating the structure factor, we note that, as given in
Eq. (475)
, the first quadrupole moments consist of two terms with different scaling behaviour with respect to the parameter we expand fluctuations about, which is the length of the spin
s
. Indeed, similarly to multi-boson expansion, or its linear spin-wave version with Holstein–Primakoff bosons or Schwinger bosons in the case of a
su(2)
representation of the spin, we assume the fluctuation to be sufficiently small compared to the spin length
s
. In other words,
C[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]
from
Eq. (470c)
and the eigenvalues in
Eq. (472)
scale with
s
as

C[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]\sim sh^{\mu}_{\textbf{q}=0\mu}\;,

(476a)

\epsilon_{\textbf{k},\lambda}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]\sim s\sqrt{Const.+\frac{h^{\mu}_{\textbf{q}=0\mu}}{s^{2}}+\frac{\mathcal{O}({\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}^{2})}{s^{2}}}\;.

(476b)

Their derivatives with respect to
h^{\mu}_{\textbf{q}=0,\mu}
that enters the quadrupole moments [
Eq. (458)
] yield

\left.\frac{C[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]}{{\partial}h^{\mu}_{\textbf{q}=0,\mu}}\right|_{\bf{h}=0}\sim s\;,

(477a)

\displaystyle\left.\frac{{\partial}\epsilon_{\textbf{k},\lambda}[{\bf{h}}_{\textbf{q}=0}]}{{\partial}h^{\mu}_{\textbf{q}=0,\mu}}\right|_{\bf{h}=0}\sim\left.\frac{s}{\sqrt{Const.}}\frac{{\partial}\frac{h^{\mu}_{\textbf{q}=0\mu}}{s^{2}}}{{\partial}h^{\mu}_{\textbf{q}=0,\mu}}\right|_{\bf{h}=0}\sim\frac{1}{s}\;.

This implies that the scaling behaviour of the first quadrupole moments goes as

{\langle Q^{\mu\mu}_{\textbf{q}=0}\rangle}_{T=0}\sim s+\frac{1}{s}\;,

(478)

where
s
is the length of the spin.
We now argue that because our approximation is valid up to linear order in
\frac{1}{s}
, i.e., second order in fluctuations, we can disregard
\frac{1}{s^{2}}
terms.
\frac{1}{s^{2}}
terms are physical but should not enter into our level of approximation. Indeed, one would expect additional contribution to the
\frac{1}{s^{2}}
term coming from higher orders in perturbation theory. However, we do not take these into account here and simply consider terms up to
\frac{1}{s}
.
According to
Section F.5
and
Eq. (469)
, the spin quadrupole structure factor at
\textbf{q}=0
yields

\displaystyle S^{\sf GS}_{\rm{Q}}(\textbf{q}=0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{8}{3}N-8\sum_{\textbf{k}}\left[\frac{A_{\textbf{k}}}{\sqrt{A_{\textbf{k}}^{2}-B_{\textbf{k}}^{2}}}\right]

(479)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{s^{2}})\;.

Its spectral representation is given by

\displaystyle S^{\sf GS}_{\rm{Q}}(\textbf{q}=0,\omega)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{8}{3}N-8\sum_{\textbf{k}}\left[\frac{A_{\textbf{k}}}{\sqrt{A_{\textbf{k}}^{2}-B_{\textbf{k}}^{2}}}\right]\right)\delta(\omega)

(480)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{s^{2}})\;.

Combining
Eq. (435)
and
Eq. (480)
, we obtain
Eq. (201)
.

F.8
A-matrices: contribution of the ground state to the quantum structure factor at
\textbf{q}=0

For the quantum zero temperature structure factor for the A–matrices at
\textbf{q}=0
, we make use of the sum rule given in
Eq. (66)
.
This leads to

\displaystyle S^{\sf GS}_{\rm{A}}(\textbf{q}=0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}S^{\sf GS}_{\rm{Q}}(\textbf{q}=0)+\frac{1}{2}S^{\sf GS}_{\rm{S}}(\textbf{q}=0)+\frac{1}{3}N\delta_{\textbf{q},0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N-2\sum_{\textbf{k}}\left[\frac{A_{\textbf{k}}}{\sqrt{A_{\textbf{k}}^{2}-B_{\textbf{k}}^{2}}}\right]+\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{s^{2}})\;,

where we used
Eq. (468)
and
Eq. (479)
.
Its spectral representation yields

\displaystyle S^{\sf GS}_{\rm{A}}(\textbf{q}=0,\omega)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(N-2\sum_{\textbf{k}}\left[\frac{A_{\textbf{k}}}{\sqrt{A_{\textbf{k}}^{2}-B_{\textbf{k}}^{2}}}\right]\right)\delta(\omega)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{O}(\frac{1}{s^{2}})\;.

Combining
Eq. (440)
with
gives the total quantum structure factor for the A-matrices expressed in
Eq. (208)

Appendix G
System size dependence of the ordered moments

We present in this Appendix, the details of the manufacturing of
Section VI.2
. More precisely, we explain how we fitted the numerical data for the ordered moments and explain how we calculated the ordered moments from the analytical results.

In
Table 1
, we show the temperature intervals on which the corresponding ordered parameters values are used for the fits of the slope
\alpha(L)
of the ordered parameters in
Fig. 14

, for different system sizes.

We also present here how the ordered moments as expressed by
Eq. (170)
and presented in
Fig. 14

are calculated . In order to compute
Eq. (170)
, we need to perform a sum in k–space. We here also show that the sum scales logarithmically with the system size
L
by explicitly calculating the coefficient
\mu
correspond to the logarithmic behavior [
Eq. (487)
]. To do this, we calculate the sum numerically for different system sizes
L
and fit it according to
Eq. (487)
(as shown by the orange line in
Fig. 14

). Additionally, we also transform the sum into an integral and extract the logarithmic scaling behaviour.

System size L

T_{min}

T_{max}

L=12

0.01

0.100177

L=24

0.01

0.100177

L=48

0.0252403

0.100177

L=96

0.0343658

0.100177

Table 1:
Temperature intervals used for fitting the parameter
\alpha(L)
according to
Eq. (218)
.

The Brillouin zone is turned into a parallelogram of area
\frac{8\pi}{\sqrt{3}}
,
as it is spanned by the reciprocal vectors
\textbf{K}_{a}
and
\textbf{K}_{b}
given in
Eq. (292)
. We then discretized it into
N=L^{2}
tiles of dimension
\delta A
given by

\begin{matrix}\delta\textbf{k}_{a}=\frac{1}{L}\textbf{K}_{a}&\;,\penalty\ &\delta\textbf{k}_{b}=\frac{1}{L}\textbf{K}_{b}\;,\end{matrix}

(483)

such that

\delta A=\frac{8\pi}{\sqrt{3}L^{2}}\;.

(484)

In order to compare numerical with analytical results, we consider

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\textbf{k}}I_{\textbf{k}}\Rightarrow N\delta A\sum_{k_{x},k_{x}}I_{\textbf{k}}=\int I_{\textbf{k}}{\rm d}\textbf{k}\;,

(485)

We can now sum over the k–space, numerically, or integrate, analytically.

In
Eq. (485)
, we take as integrant the term expressed as a sum in the result obtained in
Eq. (170)
, as we wish to compute the temperature–dependent part of the ordered moment given in
Eq. (170)
. Using
Eq. (140)
, we obtain

I_{\textbf{k}}=\frac{16A_{\textbf{k}}}{\epsilon_{\textbf{k},1}^{2}}\;,

(486)

where
\epsilon_{\textbf{k},1}
is given in
Eq. (180)
.
We then compute the discrete sum numerically according to
Eq. (485)
for the different system sizes, including the ones given in
Table 1
. When performing the sum, we also avoid the origin
\textbf{k}=(0,0)
, where
\epsilon_{\textbf{k},1}
vanishes, (indeed,
\gamma(0)=1
, and according to
Eq. (128)
,
A_{0}=B_{0}
) and which is not included in the sum of
Eq. (170)
. For a specific system size, we then get a number as the results of the discrete sum obtained for that specific system size. These numbers are plotted as the red dots in
Fig. 14

.

According to
Eq. (218)
, we assume that the system size dependency should be of the form

-\left.\frac{{\rm d}S^{CL}_{\rm{Q}}(\textbf{q}=\Gamma)}{{\rm d}T}\right|_{T=0}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\textbf{k}}I_{\textbf{k}}\sim\penalty\ C+\mu\log(L)+\frac{\nu}{L}+\frac{\xi}{L^{2}}\;.

(487)

We use
Eq. (487)
to fit the results obtained by computing the discrete sum in
Eq. (485)
, i.e, the red dots in
Fig. 14

. The fit is shown in in
Fig. 14

by the orange line.

Additionally, we want to investigate how accurate the discrete sum is, compared to the integration, and how it depends on system size.
If we consider the integral version in 2–dimensions for polar coordinates, we can cut off to some small
k_{s}=\frac{4\pi}{\sqrt{3}L}
in order to avoid the origin
\textbf{k}=(0,0)
as follows:

\int I_{\textbf{k}}{\rm d}\textbf{k}=2\pi\int_{0}^{\Lambda}I_{\textbf{k}}k{\rm d}k=2\pi\int_{0}^{k_{s}}I_{\textbf{k}}k{\rm d}k+2\pi\int_{k_{s}}^{\Lambda}I_{\textbf{k}}k{\rm d}k\;.

(488)

For the FQ state, where we chose,
J_{1}=0.0
and
J_{2}=-1.0
, the coefficients
A_{\textbf{k}}
and
B_{\textbf{k}}
[
Eq. (128)
] and the dispersion relation
\epsilon_{\textbf{k},1}
[
Eq. (180)
] become

\displaystyle A_{\textbf{k}}

\displaystyle=z\;,

\displaystyle B_{\textbf{k}}

\displaystyle=-z\gamma(\textbf{k}\;,)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\textbf{k}}^{2}

\displaystyle=z^{2}(1-\gamma(\textbf{k})^{2})\;.

(489)

For the triangular lattice, the geometrical factor is given by
Eq. (295)
, and for sufficiently small values of k, we can use the Taylor expansion on it. We obtain

\displaystyle\gamma(\textbf{k})

\displaystyle\simeq 1-\frac{1}{4}(k_{x}^{2}+k_{y}^{2})=1-\frac{1}{4}k^{2}\;,

(490a)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{\textbf{k}}^{2}

\displaystyle\simeq z^{2}\frac{1}{2}k^{2}\;,

(490b)

\displaystyle I_{\textbf{k}}=\frac{16A_{\textbf{k}}}{\epsilon_{\textbf{k},1}^{2}}

\displaystyle\simeq\frac{16z}{z^{2}\frac{1}{2}k^{2}}=\frac{32}{zk^{2}}\;,

(490c)

\displaystyle 2\pi\int_{k_{s}}^{k_{f}}I_{\textbf{k}}k{\rm d}k

\displaystyle\simeq 2\pi\int_{k_{s}}^{k_{f}}\frac{32}{zk^{2}}k{\rm d}k\;.

(490d)

Since
z=6
for the triangular lattice, we have

\displaystyle\frac{2\pi 16}{3}\int_{k_{s}}^{k_{f}}\frac{1}{k}{\rm d}k=\frac{2\pi 16}{3}(\log(k_{f})-\log(k_{s}))\;,

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi 16}{3}\log(L)+\frac{2\pi 16}{3}\log(k_{f})-\frac{2\pi 16}{3}\log(\frac{4\pi}{\sqrt{3}})\;,

(491)

where in the last line, we used the fact that we chose to cut off according to
k_{s}=\frac{4\pi}{\sqrt{3}L}
.
Before we fit the sum with the expression given by
Eq. (487)
, we need to account for correction coming from the tiling of the k-space as explained in
Eq. (485)
. Therefore, we need to divide by

\delta A*N=\frac{8\pi^{2}}{\sqrt{3}}\;.

(492)

From
Eq. (487)
, we can obtain the value for the coefficient
\mu
for
Eq. (491)
, which we can compare with the fit from the values of the sum calculated numerically as shown in
Fig. 14

:

\begin{matrix}\mu_{ana}=\frac{\frac{2\pi 16}{3}}{\frac{8\pi^{2}}{\sqrt{3}}}=\frac{4}{\sqrt{3}\pi}=0.735&\;,\penalty\ &\mu_{num}=0.735\;.\end{matrix}

(493)

Appendix H
Useful Gaussian integrals

We present here useful Gaussian integrals that we used to calculate partition functions for the analytic derivations.
We namely used the following one–dimensional Gaussian integrals

\displaystyle\int e^{-\frac{1}{2}ax^{2}}{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{a}}\;,

(494a)

\displaystyle\int e^{-\frac{1}{2}ax^{2}}e^{\pm bx}{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{a}}e^{\frac{b^{2}}{2a}}\;,

(494b)

\displaystyle\int e^{-\frac{1}{2}ax^{2}}e^{\pm ibx}{\rm d}x

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{a}}e^{-\frac{b^{2}}{2a}}\;,

(494c)

which we can also generalize to a multi-dimensions integral with a source term

\int e^{-\frac{1}{2}x_{i}A_{ij}x_{j}+B_{i}x_{i}}{\rm d}\vec{x}=\sqrt{\frac{(2\pi)^{n}}{\det A}}e^{\frac{1}{2}\vec{B}^{T}A^{-1}\vec{B}}\;.

(494d)

or more generally,

\int e^{-\frac{1}{2}x_{i}{A^{i}}_{j}x^{j}+B_{1,i}^{T}x^{i}+x_{i}B_{2}^{i}}{\rm d}\vec{x}=\sqrt{\frac{(2\pi)^{n}}{\det A}}e^{2\vec{B}_{1}^{T}A^{-1}\vec{B}_{2}}\;.

(494e)

where
n
is the dimension of of the matrix
\bf{A}
.
Below, we give the proof for
Eq. (494e)
.

Proof:

We assume
A
to be a real symmetric
n\times n
-matrix. This means that
A
is orthogonally diagonalizable, i.e., it is similar to a diagonal matrix
\textbf{D}={\rm diag}(d^{1}_{\penalty\ 1},\dots,d^{n}_{\penalty\ n})

\textbf{A}=\textbf{S}\textbf{D}\textbf{S}^{-1}\;,

(495)

and the
n\times n
basis change matrix
S
is orthogonal.
The basis change matrix
S
then satisfies

\textbf{S}^{T}=\textbf{S}^{-1}\;,

(496)

and the old coordinates are related to the new ones by

\displaystyle\vec{x}

\displaystyle=\textbf{S}\vec{y}\;,

(497a)

\displaystyle\vec{x}^{T}

\displaystyle=\vec{y}\textbf{S}^{T}\;,

(497b)

\displaystyle{\rm d}\vec{x}

\displaystyle=\det\textbf{S}{\rm d}\vec{y}={\rm d}\vec{y}\;,

(497c)

where in the last line we used the fact that the Jacobian matrix of the map
\vec{x}(\vec{y})\rightarrow\textbf{S}\vec{y}
is the matrix
S
itself, and that its determinant is 1, since it is an orthogonal matrix.
The term in the exponential in
Eq. (494e)
then becomes

\displaystyle E:=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\vec{x}^{T}\textbf{A}\vec{x}+\vec{B}_{1}^{T}\vec{x}+\vec{x}^{T}\vec{B}_{2}

(498a)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\vec{y}^{T}\textbf{D}\vec{y}+\vec{B}_{1}^{T}\textbf{S}\vec{y}+\vec{y}^{T}\textbf{S}^{-1}\vec{B}_{2}\;.

(498b)

If we expand, we obtain:

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\left[-\frac{1}{2}y_{i}d_{\penalty\ i}^{i}y^{i}+\sum_{\alpha}\left(B_{1,\alpha}^{T}S^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ i}y^{i}+y_{i}(S^{-1})^{i}_{\penalty\ \alpha}B_{2}^{\alpha}\right)\right]\;,

(498c)

where we used the fact that
D
is a diagonal matrix.
For the
i^{th}
term, we can complete the square as

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}y_{i}d_{\penalty\ i}^{i}y^{i}+\sum_{\alpha}\left(B_{1,\alpha}^{T}S^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ i}y^{i}+y_{i}(S^{-1})^{i}_{\penalty\ \alpha}B_{2}^{i}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}d_{i}^{i}(y_{i}-\frac{2}{d_{\penalty\ i}^{i}}\sum_{\alpha}B_{1,\alpha}^{T}S^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ i})(y^{i}-\frac{2}{d_{i}^{i}}\sum_{\beta}(S^{-1})^{i}_{\penalty\ \beta}B_{2}^{\beta})

\displaystyle+2\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{\beta}B_{1,\alpha}^{T}S^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ i}\frac{1}{d_{i}^{i}}(S^{-1})^{i}_{\penalty\ \beta}B_{2}^{\beta}\;.

(499)

Using again the fact that
d_{\penalty\ i}^{i}
is the
i^{th}
diagonal term of
D
, and inverting
Eq. (495)
, we can rewrite
\frac{1}{d_{i}^{i}}
as

\frac{1}{d_{i}^{i}}=(D^{-1})^{i}_{\penalty\ i}=\sum_{\mu,\nu}(S^{-1})^{i}_{\penalty\ \mu}(A^{-1})^{\mu}_{\penalty\ \nu}(S)^{\nu}_{\penalty\ i}\;.

(500)

Performing the variable change as

\displaystyle z_{i}

\displaystyle=y_{i}-\frac{2}{d_{\penalty\ i}^{i}}\sum_{\alpha}B_{1,\alpha}^{T}S^{\alpha}_{\penalty\ i}\;,

(501a)

\displaystyle z^{i}

\displaystyle=y^{i}-\frac{2}{d_{i}^{i}}\sum_{\beta}(S^{-1})^{i}_{\penalty\ \beta}B_{2}^{\beta}\;,

(501b)

\displaystyle{\rm d}\vec{z}

\displaystyle={\rm d}\vec{y}\;,

(501c)

and inserting
Eq. (500)
into the last term of
Eq. (499)
, and summing over all the components, we obtain

\displaystyle E=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\vec{z}^{T}\textbf{D}\vec{z}+2\vec{B}_{1}^{T}(\textbf{A}^{-1})\vec{B}_{2}\;.

(502a)

We now have the product of n Gaussian integrals of the form of
Eq. (494a)

\displaystyle\int

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{1}{2}x_{i}{A^{i}}_{j}x^{j}+B_{1,i}^{T}x^{i}+x_{i}B_{2}^{i}}{\rm d}\vec{x}=

\displaystyle=\left[\prod_{i}\int e^{-\frac{1}{2}z_{i}{d^{i}}_{i}z^{i}}{\rm d}z_{i}\right]e^{2\vec{B}_{1}^{T}(\textbf{A}^{-1})\vec{B}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{(2\pi)^{n}}{\prod_{i}{d^{i}}_{\penalty\ i}}}e^{2\vec{B}_{1}^{T}(\textbf{A}^{-1})\vec{B}_{2}}\;.

(503)

We then use the fact that

\det\textbf{A}=\det\textbf{S}\det\textbf{D}\det\textbf{S}^{-1}=\det\textbf{D}=\prod_{i}{d^{i}}_{\penalty\ i}\;,

(504)

to obtain
Eq. (494e)

Appendix I
Application to an easy–plane ferromagnet

We here wish to apply the
u(3)
formalism and its representation in terms of the A-matrices to the Heisenberg ferromagnetic easy-plane anisotropic model.
The A-matrices are especially useful to work with on the TR-invariant basis, and relatively easy to use when the ground state is quadrupolar. However, some attention is demanded when working with systems where dipoles rather than quadrupoles order. We demonstrate here how one can carefully apply our method for dipolar ordering. Additionally, as explained in
Section VIII
, we make the interactions anisotropic.
We show results for the zero–temperature quantum structure factors for dipole, quadrupole and A-matrix moments applied to the ferromagnetic (FM) state on the triangular lattice for the anisotropic Heisenberg Hamiltonian (BBQ Hamiltonian [
Eq. (1)
] with anisotropic
J_{1}
and
J_{2}=0
), with single–ion anisotropy.

Figure 22:

Eigenstates of
{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{x}}
and basis states of
\mathcal{B}^{x}

such that

\displaystyle{\left\langle 1^{x}\right|}{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{x}}{\left|1^{x}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=1\;,

(505a)

\displaystyle{\left\langle 0^{x}\right|}{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{x}}{\left|0^{x}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=0\;,

(505b)

\displaystyle{\left\langle\overline{1^{x}}\right|}{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{x}}{\left|\overline{1^{x}}\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=-1\;,

(505c)

\displaystyle{\left\langle\alpha\right|}{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{\mu}}{\left|\alpha\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=0\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \textrm{for }{\left|\alpha\right\rangle}\in\mathcal{B}^{x}\textrm{ and }\mu=y,z\;.

(505d)

We consider the following
Hamiltonian

{\cal H}={\cal H}^{\rm{EP}}+{\cal H}^{\rm{SI}}\;.

(506)

{\cal H}^{\rm{EP}}
represents the Heisenberg Hamiltonian for spin-1 with easy–plane Heisenberg anisotropic exchange couplings
J

{\cal H}^{\rm{EP}}=\sum_{{\langle i,j\rangle}}\left[{\boldsymbol{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}}}\cdot\textbf{J}\cdot{\boldsymbol{\hat{\rm{S}}_{j}}}\right]\;,

(507)

where the spin dipole operator
{\boldsymbol{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}}}
is defined in
Eq. (2)
, and where
J
corresponds to the usual nearest neighbor spin-spin coupling tensor.

{\cal H}^{\rm{SI}}
accounts for single–ion anisotropy and is given by

{\cal H}^{\rm{SI}}=\sum_{i}{\boldsymbol{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}}}\textbf{D}{\boldsymbol{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}}}\;,

(508)

where
D
corresponds to the usual single site spin-spin coupling tensor.
We assume the spin-spin coupling tensors
J
and
D
to only have diagonal components:

{\cal H}^{\rm{EP}}=\sum_{{\langle i,j\rangle}}\left[J^{xx}{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{x}}{\hat{\rm{S}}_{j}^{x}}+J^{yy}{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{y}}{\hat{\rm{S}}_{j}^{y}}+J^{zz}{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{z}}{\hat{\rm{S}}_{j}^{z}}\right]\;,

(509)

{\cal H}^{\rm{SI}}=\sum_{i}\left[D^{xx}{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{x}}{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{x}}+D^{yy}{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{y}}{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{y}}+D^{zz}{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{z}}{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{z}}\right]\;.

(510)

We also assume the coupling constants to be negative and the order to be ferromagnetic:

J^{\alpha\alpha}<0\;,

(511)

D^{\alpha\alpha}<0\;.

(512)

We can assume the ground state to be a state with the spin pointing somewhere in the xy-plane, and we can choose it to be pointing along the x-axis:

{\left|GS\right\rangle}={\left|1^{x}\right\rangle}\;.

(513)

As a basis, we choose the eigenstates of
{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{x}}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}^{x}

\displaystyle=\left\{{\left|1^{x}\right\rangle},{\left|0^{x}\right\rangle},{\left|\overline{1^{x}}\right\rangle}\right\}\;,

(514)

as represented in
Fig. 22
,

Even though the A-matrices are deeply linked to the time-reversal (TR) invariant basis, we will here mostly focus on the basis
\mathcal{B}^{x}
[
Eq. (514)
] and then transform the required quantities accordingly.

Figure 23:

Fluctuations created by the generators
{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\penalty\ y}^{x}}
,
{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\penalty\ z}^{x}}
,
{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\penalty\ x}^{y}}
, and
{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\penalty\ x}^{z}}
, according to
Eq. (117)
, for an angle
\phi_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{\pi}{8}
.

To do that, we remember that the spin dipole moments can be rewritten in terms of the A matrices [
Eq. (50)
], expressed in the time-reversal (TR) invariant basis [
Eq. (36)
] as shown in
Fig. 5
.
Using
Eq. (50)
, the terms of the easy–plane anisotropic Hamiltonian [
Eq. (509)
], in terms of the A-matrices, becomes

\displaystyle{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{x}}{\hat{\rm{S}}_{j}^{x}}

\displaystyle=-({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ z}^{y}}-{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ y}^{z}})({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ z}^{y}}-{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ y}^{z}})\;,

(515a)

\displaystyle{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{y}}{\hat{\rm{S}}_{j}^{y}}

\displaystyle=-({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ x}^{z}}-{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ z}^{x}})({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ x}^{z}}-{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ z}^{x}})\;,

(515b)

\displaystyle{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{z}}{\hat{\rm{S}}_{j}^{z}}

\displaystyle=({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ y}^{x}}-{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ x}^{y}})({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ y}^{x}}-{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ x}^{y}})\;.

(515c)

For the single ion terms, we use
Eq. (26)
and
Eq. (51)
to rewrite the terms
{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{\alpha}}{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{\alpha}}
of the single–ion anisotropic Hamiltonian [
Eq. (510)
] in the function of the A-matrices, as

\displaystyle{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{x}}{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{x}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{3}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ x}^{x}}+\frac{1}{3}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ y}^{y}}+\frac{1}{3}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ z}^{z}}+\frac{2}{3}\;,

(516a)

\displaystyle{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{y}}{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{y}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{3}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ y}^{y}}+\frac{1}{3}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ x}^{x}}+\frac{1}{3}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ z}^{z}}+\frac{2}{3}\;,

(516b)

\displaystyle{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{z}}{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{z}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{3}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ z}^{z}}+\frac{1}{3}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ y}^{y}}+\frac{1}{3}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ x}^{x}}+\frac{2}{3}\;.

(516c)

Using
Eq. (515)
and
Eq. (516)
, the total Hamiltonian [
Eq. (506)
] in terms of the A-matrices then becomes

\displaystyle{\cal H}=\sum_{{\langle i,j\rangle}}

\displaystyle\left[-J^{xx}({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ z}^{y}}-{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ y}^{z}})({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ z}^{y}}-{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ y}^{z}}))\right.

\displaystyle\left.-J^{yy}({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ x}^{z}}-{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ z}^{x}})({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ x}^{z}}-{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ z}^{x}})\right.

\displaystyle\left.-J^{zz}({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ y}^{x}}-{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ x}^{y}})({\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ y}^{x}}-{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{j\penalty\ x}^{y}})\right]

\displaystyle+\sum_{i}

\displaystyle\left[D^{xx}(-\frac{2}{3}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ x}^{x}}+\frac{1}{3}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ y}^{y}}+\frac{1}{3}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ z}^{z}}+\frac{2}{3})\right.

\displaystyle\left.+D^{yy}(-\frac{2}{3}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ y}^{y}}+\frac{1}{3}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ x}^{x}}+\frac{1}{3}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ z}^{z}}+\frac{2}{3})\right.

\displaystyle\left.+D^{zz}(-\frac{2}{3}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ z}^{z}}+\frac{1}{3}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ y}^{y}}+\frac{1}{3}{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{i\penalty\ x}^{x}}+\frac{2}{3})\right]\;.

(517)

If we define the basis change
\Lambda_{3}
to be the basis change matrix between
\mathcal{B}_{2}
and
\mathcal{B}^{x}
, such that if a state
{\left|\phi\right\rangle}_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}
is given in the TR invariant basis
\mathcal{B}_{2}
, in the basis
\mathcal{B}^{x}
, its components are given by

{\left|\phi\right\rangle}_{\mathcal{B}^{x}}=\Lambda_{3}{\left|\phi\right\rangle}_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}\;.

(518)

We found that the basis change matrix
\Lambda_{3}
yields

\Lambda_{3}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}&\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}\\
-i&0&0\\
0&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}&-\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}\end{pmatrix}\;.

(519)

An operator
{\hat{\mathrm{O}}}_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}
given in the TR invariant basis
\mathcal{B}_{2}

is expressed as

{\hat{\mathrm{O}}}_{\mathcal{B}^{x}}=\Lambda_{3}{\hat{\mathrm{O}}}_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}\Lambda_{3}^{\dagger}\;,

(520)

in the basis
\mathcal{B}^{x}
.

We will start working the basis
\mathcal{B}^{x}
, where everything is simple, since the ground state is one of the basis states and the orthogonal fluctuations can be expressed in terms of the other orthogonal basis states.
Indeed, the ground state matrix takes the simple form

{\bf{A}_{0}}_{\mathcal{B}^{x}}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
0&0&0\\
\end{pmatrix}\;,

(521)

since the ground state is simply the state
{\left|1^{x}\right\rangle}
[
Eq. (513)
]
or expressed in terms of director components

{\textbf{d}^{\dagger}_{0}}_{\mathcal{B}^{x}}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}\;.

(522)

We can generate orthogonal fluctuations by application of the exponential map given in
Eq. (117)
.
The new state describing the fluctuations around the ground state is given by

{\textbf{d}^{\dagger}}(\phi)=\hat{R}(\phi){\textbf{d}^{\dagger}_{0}}\;.

(523)

The A matrix transforms according to
Eq. (118)
.
Only the generators
{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\penalty\ x}^{x}}
,
{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\penalty\ y}^{x}}
,
{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\penalty\ z}^{x}}
,
{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\penalty\ x}^{y}}
, and
{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\penalty\ x}^{z}}
will have non zero contribution when applied to the ground state matrix [
Eq. (521)
].
Fig. 23
represents the action of the generators on the ground state. We can see, for example, that the generator
{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\penalty\ y}^{x}}
, will create a fluctuation along
{\left|0^{x}\right\rangle}
, i.e. an
{\hat{\rm{a}}^{\dagger}}
boson, and will induce the new state to exhibit some quadrupolar features.

Using the constraint on the trace of A-matrices [
Eq. (49)
], we express the contribution from
{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\penalty\ x}^{x}}
in terms of the others components in order to ensure the length of the spin to be
S=1
(which is equivalent to constraining the trace of A to be equal to 1), so that we properly restrict to
su(3)
and make sure that we are correctly representing a spin-1. We obtain

\displaystyle\textbf{A}(\phi)_{\mathcal{B}^{x}}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}1-\phi_{xy}\phi_{yx}-\phi_{xz}\phi_{zx}&i\phi_{xy}&i\phi_{xz}\\
-i\phi_{yx}&\phi_{xy}\phi_{yx}&\phi_{xz}\phi_{yx}\\
-i\phi_{zx}&\phi_{xy}\phi_{zx}&\phi_{xz}\phi_{zx}\\
\end{pmatrix}\;.

(524)

We can then easily introduce bosonic fluctuations by

\displaystyle i\phi_{xy}

\displaystyle={\hat{\rm{a}}}\;,

(525a)

\displaystyle-i\phi_{yx}

\displaystyle={\hat{\rm{a}}^{\dagger}}\;,

(525b)

\displaystyle i\phi_{xz}

\displaystyle={\hat{\rm{b}}}\;,

(525c)

\displaystyle-i\phi_{zx}

\displaystyle={\hat{\rm{b}}^{\dagger}}\;,

(525d)

such that we get

{\boldsymbol{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}}_{\mathcal{B}^{x}}=\begin{pmatrix}1-{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}^{\dagger}}{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}}-{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}^{\dagger}}{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}}&{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}}&{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}}\\
{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}^{\dagger}}&{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}^{\dagger}}{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}}&{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}^{\dagger}}{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}}\\
{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}^{\dagger}}&{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}}{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}^{\dagger}}&{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}^{\dagger}}{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}}\end{pmatrix}\;.

(526)

According to
Eq. (520)
, the A matrices expressed in the TR invariant basis
\mathcal{B}_{2}
are given by

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}}_{B_{2}}

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{3}^{\dagger}{\boldsymbol{\hat{\mathrm{A}}}}_{\mathcal{B}^{x}}\Lambda_{3}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}^{\dagger}}{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}}&\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}^{\dagger}}+\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}^{\dagger}}{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}}&-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}^{\dagger}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}^{\dagger}}{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}}\\
-\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}}-\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}}{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}^{\dagger}}&\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}^{\dagger}}+\frac{1}{2}{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}}-\frac{1}{2}{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}^{\dagger}}{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}}&\frac{i}{2}+\frac{i}{2}{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}^{\dagger}}-\frac{i}{2}{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}}-\frac{i}{2}{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}^{\dagger}}{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}}-i{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}^{\dagger}}{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}}\\
-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}}{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}^{\dagger}}&-\frac{i}{2}-\frac{i}{2}{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}}+\frac{i}{2}{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}^{\dagger}}+\frac{i}{2}{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}^{\dagger}}{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}}+i{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}^{\dagger}}{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}}&\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}^{\dagger}}-\frac{1}{2}{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}}-\frac{1}{2}{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}^{\dagger}}{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}}\end{pmatrix}\;.

(527)

Inserting
Eq. (527)
into
Eq. (516)
, we get the single–ion terms in the function of the bosons

\displaystyle{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{x}}{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{x}}

\displaystyle=1-{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}^{\dagger}}{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}}\;,

\displaystyle{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{y}}{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{y}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(1+{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}^{\dagger}}{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}}-{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}^{\dagger}}-{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}})\;,

(528)

\displaystyle{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{z}}{\hat{\rm{S}}_{i}^{z}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(1+{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}^{\dagger}}{\hat{\rm{a}}_{i}}+{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}^{\dagger}}+{\hat{\rm{b}}_{i}})\;.

We notice that if
D^{yy}
is not equal to
D^{zz}
, then the Hamiltonian [
Eq. (517)
] has single bosons terms, meaning that the state about which we expanded the fluctuations is not the ground state any more.
Therefore, to be consistent with the easy-plane FM order and the ground state [
Eq. (513)
], we choose

D^{\perp}=D^{yy}=D^{zz}\textrm{\penalty\ with\penalty\ }{\left|D^{\perp}\right|}<{\left|D^{xx}\right|}\;.

(529)

After inserting
Eq. (527)
into the total Hamiltonian [
Eq. (517)
], only keeping fluctuations
up to 2nd order, and performing a Fourier transform, the Hamiltonian [
Eq. (517)
] becomes

\displaystyle{\cal H}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sum_{{\textbf{k}}}\left[\begin{pmatrix}{\hat{\rm{a}}_{{\textbf{k}}}^{\dagger}},&{\hat{\rm{a}}_{-{\textbf{k}}}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}A_{\textbf{k}}&B_{\textbf{k}}\\
B_{\textbf{k}}&A_{\textbf{k}}\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{\hat{\rm{a}}_{{\textbf{k}}}}\\
{\hat{\rm{a}}_{-{\textbf{k}}}^{\dagger}}\\
\end{pmatrix}\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\begin{pmatrix}{\hat{\rm{b}}_{{\textbf{k}}}^{\dagger}},&{\hat{\rm{b}}_{-{\textbf{k}}}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}C_{\textbf{k}}&0\\
0&C_{\textbf{k}}\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}{\hat{\rm{b}}_{{\textbf{k}}}}\\
{\hat{\rm{b}}_{-{\textbf{k}}}^{\dagger}}\\
\end{pmatrix}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}NzJ^{xx}+N(D^{xx}+D^{\perp})\;,

(530)

where

\begin{matrix}A_{\textbf{k}}=-J^{xx}z+\frac{1}{2}z(J^{yy}+J^{zz})\gamma(\textbf{k})+(D^{\perp}-D^{xx})\;,\\
B_{\textbf{k}}=\frac{1}{2}z(J^{zz}-J^{yy})\gamma(\textbf{k})\;,\\
C_{\textbf{k}}=-2zJ^{xx}\;.\end{matrix}

(531)

Similarly to the FQ case, we need to solve an eigensystem analogous to
Eq. (178)
. The dispersion relations for
{\hat{\rm{a}}_{\textbf{k}}^{\dagger}}
and
{\hat{\rm{a}}_{\textbf{k}}}
can be found by imposing them to have bosonic commutation relations [
Eq. (382)
], and diagonalizing

\sigma_{z}\begin{pmatrix}A_{\textbf{k}}&B_{\textbf{k}}\\
B_{\textbf{k}}&A_{\textbf{k}}\\
\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}A_{\textbf{k}}&B_{\textbf{k}}\\
B_{\textbf{k}}&A_{\textbf{k}}\\
\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}A_{\textbf{k}}&B_{\textbf{k}}\\
-B_{\textbf{k}}&-A_{\textbf{k}}\\
\end{pmatrix}\;,

(532)

where the multiplication by
\sigma_{z}
imposes the bosonic commutation relations.
The eigenvalues
\epsilon_{\textbf{k}}
are given by

\begin{matrix}\epsilon_{\textbf{k},1}=+\sqrt{A_{\textbf{k}}^{2}-B_{\textbf{k}}^{2}}&,\penalty\ &\epsilon_{\textbf{k},2}=-\sqrt{A_{\textbf{k}}^{2}-B_{\textbf{k}}^{2}}\end{matrix}\;.

(533)

The dispersion relations for the
{\hat{\rm{b}}_{\textbf{k}}^{\dagger}}
and
{\hat{\rm{b}}_{\textbf{k}}}
are obtained by diagonalizing

\sigma_{z}\begin{pmatrix}C_{\textbf{k}}&0\\
0&C_{\textbf{k}}\\
\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}C_{\textbf{k}}&0\\
0&-C_{\textbf{k}}\\
\end{pmatrix}\;.

(534)

The eigenvalues
\epsilon_{\textbf{k}}
are given by

\begin{matrix}\epsilon_{\textbf{k},3}=-C_{\textbf{k}}&,\penalty\ &\epsilon_{\textbf{k},4}=+C_{\textbf{k}}\end{matrix}\;.

(535)

Because the coupling constants are negative, the physical results are

\begin{matrix}\epsilon_{\textbf{k},1}=+\sqrt{A_{\textbf{k}}^{2}-B_{\textbf{k}}^{2}}&,\penalty\ &\epsilon_{\textbf{k},3}=-C_{\textbf{k}}=2z{\left|J^{xx}\right|}\end{matrix}\;,

(536)

where
A_{\textbf{k}}
,
B_{\textbf{k}}
, and
C_{\textbf{k}}
are given in
Eq. (531)
.

Following the same procedure as for the FQ state in
Section V
, we calculate dynamical structure factors for the anisotropic FM case. We start by finding the Bogoliubov transformation that diagonalizes
Eq. (530)
. Following the steps given in
Appendix
E
, we get

\displaystyle{\hat{\rm{a}}_{\textbf{k}}}{}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Delta_{\textbf{k}}^{2}-B_{\textbf{k}}^{2}}}(\Delta_{\textbf{k}}{\hat{\alpha}_{\textbf{k}}}-B_{\textbf{k}}{\hat{\alpha}_{-\textbf{k}}^{\dagger}})\;,

(537a)

\displaystyle{\hat{\rm{a}}_{-\textbf{k}}^{\dagger}}{}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Delta_{\textbf{k}}^{2}-B_{\textbf{k}}^{2}}}(-B_{\textbf{k}}{\hat{\alpha}_{\textbf{k}}}+\Delta_{\textbf{k}}{\hat{\alpha}_{-\textbf{k}}^{\dagger}})\;,

(537b)

\displaystyle{\hat{\rm{a}}_{\textbf{k}}^{\dagger}}{}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Delta_{\textbf{k}}^{2}-B_{\textbf{k}}^{2}}}(\Delta_{\textbf{k}}{\hat{\alpha}_{\textbf{k}}^{\dagger}}-B_{\textbf{k}}{\hat{\alpha}_{-\textbf{k}}})\;,

(537c)

\displaystyle{\hat{\rm{a}}_{-\textbf{k}}}{}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{\Delta_{\textbf{k}}^{2}-B_{\textbf{k}}^{2}}}(-B_{\textbf{k}}{\hat{\alpha}_{\textbf{k}}^{\dagger}}+\Delta_{\textbf{k}}{\hat{\alpha}_{-\textbf{k}}})\;,

(537d)

and

\displaystyle{\hat{\rm{b}}_{\textbf{k}}}{}

\displaystyle={\hat{\beta}_{\textbf{k}}}\;,

(538a)

\displaystyle{\hat{\rm{b}}_{-\textbf{k}}^{\dagger}}{}

\displaystyle={\hat{\beta}_{-\textbf{k}}^{\dagger}}\;,

(538b)

\displaystyle{\hat{\rm{b}}_{\textbf{k}}^{\dagger}}{}

\displaystyle={\hat{\beta}_{\textbf{k}}^{\dagger}}\;,

(538c)

\displaystyle{\hat{\rm{b}}_{-\textbf{k}}}{}

\displaystyle={\hat{\beta}_{-\textbf{k}}}\;,

(538d)

where
\Delta_{\textbf{k}}
is given in
Eq. (183)
, and where
A_{\textbf{k}}
and
B_{\textbf{k}}
are given in
Eq. (531)
.

We follow now the calculations outlined in
Section F.1
in order to calculate the quantum structure factors.
Since we are working in the Bogoliubov representation, the ground state
{\left|\rm{GS}\right\rangle}
is the vacuum state
{\left|\rm{vac}\right\rangle}
for the Bogoliubov bosons. The structure factors are given by
Eq. (187)
. We calculate
{\left\langle\rm{vac}\right|}\hat{O}_{\textbf{q}}^{\alpha}{\left|\mu\right\rangle}
with
{\left|\mu\right\rangle}={\hat{\alpha}_{\textbf{k}}^{\dagger}}{\left|\rm{vac}\right\rangle}\oplus{\hat{\beta}_{\textbf{k}}^{\dagger}}{\left|\rm{vac}\right\rangle}
and

\hat{O}_{\textbf{q}}^{\alpha}={\hat{\rm{S}}_{\textbf{k}}^{\alpha}}
with
\alpha=x,y,z
, for the dipole structure factor for instance.
Using
Eq. (50)
,
Eq. (51)
and
Eq. (527)
, we can rewrite the spin dipole, the spin quadropole, and the A-matrix operators in terms of the bosons up to linear order, and after performing a Fourier transform, we can rewrite them in terms of the Bogoliubov bosons using
Eq. (537)
and
Eq. (538)
. This allows to easily calculate the structure factors [
Eq. (187)
].

(a)
\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ S^{\sf QM}_{\rm{A}}(\textbf{q},\omega)\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\

\left(\right.J^{xx}=J^{yy}=J^{zz}\left.\right)

(b)
\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ S^{\sf QM}_{\rm{Q}}(\textbf{q},\omega)\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\

\left(\right.J^{xx}=J^{yy}=J^{zz}\left.\right)

(c)
\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ S^{\sf QM}_{\rm{S}}(\textbf{q},\omega)\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\

\left(\right.J^{xx}=J^{yy}=J^{zz}\left.\right)

for

.

[

.

Using
Eq. (419)
, the dynamical spin dipole structure factor, defined by
Eq. (424)
, is given by

\displaystyle S^{\sf FM}_{\rm{S}}(\textbf{q},\omega)

\displaystyle=\frac{A_{\textbf{q}}}{\sqrt{A_{\textbf{q}}^{2}-B_{\textbf{q}}^{2}}}\delta(\omega-\epsilon_{\textbf{q},1})+S^{\sf GS_{\sf{FM}}}_{\rm{S}}(\textbf{q}=0,\omega)\;.

(539)

The dynamical spin quadrupole structure factor, as given by
Eq. (431)
, yields

\displaystyle S^{\sf FM}_{\rm{Q}}(\textbf{q},\omega)

\displaystyle=2\frac{A_{\textbf{q}}}{\sqrt{A_{\textbf{q}}^{2}-B_{\textbf{q}}^{2}}}\delta(\omega-\epsilon_{\textbf{q},1})+4\delta(\omega-\epsilon_{\textbf{q},3})

\displaystyle+S^{\sf GS_{\sf{FM}}}_{\rm{Q}}(\textbf{q}=0,\omega)\;.

(540)

The total dynamical factor for the
{\hat{\mathcal{A}}_{\penalty\ }}
operators defined in
Eq. (436)
becomes

\displaystyle S_{\rm{A}}^{\sf FM}(\textbf{q},\omega)

\displaystyle=\frac{A_{\textbf{q}}}{\sqrt{A_{\textbf{q}}^{2}-B_{\textbf{q}}^{2}}}\delta(\omega-\epsilon_{\textbf{q},1})+\delta(\omega-\epsilon_{\textbf{q},3})

\displaystyle+S^{\sf GS_{\sf{FM}}}_{\rm{A}}(\textbf{q}=0,\omega)\;,

(541)

where we explicitly summed over the indexes
\alpha
and
\beta
and where the terms of the form
S^{\sf GS_{\sf{FM}}}_{\rm{O}}(\textbf{q}=0,\omega)
represent the ground state and zero–point energy contribution to the structure factors at
\textbf{q}=0
, but are not calculated here, for simplicity reasons.
For these 3 results,
Eq. (539)
,
Eq. (540)
, and
Eq. (541)
, we used
Eq. (183)
, and
\epsilon_{\textbf{q},1}
, and
\epsilon_{\textbf{q},3}
are given in
Eq. (536)
.

We also check that the sum rule
Eq. (66)
is indeed satisfied after noticing that the constant terms in
Eq. (66)
would only contribute for
\textbf{q}=0
and at equal time, and can therefore be neglected.
These results are identical to results that one can obtain by performing a conventional multi-bosons expansion.

In
Fig. 24
, we show results for the dynamical structure factors [
Eq. (539)
,
Eq. (540)
, and
Eq. (541)
] for the ferromagnetic state for the anisotropic Heisenberg Hamiltonian with single–ion anisotropy [
Eq. (506)
] on the triangular lattice.
We first notice that the quadrupolar band
\epsilon_{\textbf{q},3}
, which corresponds to the
\Delta S=2
excitation band associated with the
{\hat{\beta}_{\textbf{k}}^{\dagger}}
boson, is gapped and non–dispersive. Because it essentially corresponds to the excitation obtained by applying the lowering operator
S^{+}
twice, it is quadrupolar in nature and will only contribute to the quadrupolar structure factor channel. Moreover, such a quadrupolar excitation from a FM ground state has a finite energy cost, and it also doesn’t have any neighboring quadrupoles to interact with, so it is therefore localized.
The isotropic FM Heisenberg case without single–ion anisotropy is presented in
Fig. 24

–
.
As shown in
Fig. 24

–
, we note that the introduction of easy–plane anisotropy with
J^{yy}=J^{zz}\neq J^{xx}
creates a gap and lifts the dispersion relation according to
Eq. (536)
and
Eq. (531)
.
In
Fig. 24

–
, we see that introducing single–ion anisotropy with
D^{\perp}\neq D^{xx}
also creates a gap and lifts the dispersion relation again according to
Eq. (536)
and
Eq. (531)
.
In
Fig. 24

–
, we display the interplay of easy-plane and single–ion anisotropy.
